     THE SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY WITH CHARGE AND
                   ORIGINAL-DATA RIGIDITY

                                                   OPENAI


     Abstract. Under the stated energy, decay, and trapping hypotheses, we prove the sharp charged
     spacetime Penrose upper-area inequality for one-ended three-dimensional initial data with source-
     free electric and magnetic fields. The theorem allows arbitrary second fundamental form, nonzero
     ADM momentum, and disconnected boundary. Writing m for invariant ADM mass, Q for total
     charge magnitude,
                 p       and rA for the minimum-enclosing-area radius, the bound is m ≥ Q and
     rA ≤ m + m2 − Q2 . The polynomial mass bound m ≥ (rA + Q2 /rA )/2 is asserted only when
     rA > Q. When m > Q, equality under the stated connected, outermost, outer-area-minimizing
     future-horizon hypotheses identifies the original data as a smooth spacelike slice of dyonic Reissner–
     Nordström, including smooth attachment at the future horizon.

                                        1. Introduction
   Penrose’s mass–area inequality connects the geometry of initial data to the expected evolution of
blackp holes under weak cosmic censorship [18]. In the absence of charge, its characteristic form is
m ≥ A/(16π). In time-symmetric data, where the scalar curvature is nonnegative and horizons
are minimal surfaces, Huisken and Ilmanen proved the three-dimensional bound using the area of
any connected component of an outermost minimal boundary, and Bray proved it using the total
area of a possibly disconnected outer minimizing minimal boundary [12, 6]. The passage to an
arbitrary second fundamental form requires an area quantity attached to the original spacelike
exterior. The minimum-area enclosure in the spacetime formulation of Bray and Khuri motivates
the full-cut quantity used here [7]. This formulation differs from bounds using the apparent-horizon
area itself or the area of an outermost generalized apparent horizon, for which counterexamples are
known [4, 8].
   Charge changes both the inequality
                                    p       and its equality model. If Q2 = Q2E + Q2B , the outer
Reissner–Nordström radius is m + m2 − Q2 . The appropriate statement for possibly disconnected
horizons is the upper-area inequality
                                           s
                                               A       q
                               m ≥ Q,             ≤ m + m2 − Q2 .                              (1.1)
                                               4π
                            CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                    3


Writing rA = A/(4π), the familiar polynomial expression m ≥ (rA + Q2 /rA )/2 is the required
              p

branch only for rA > Q. Khuri, Weinstein and Yamada developed a charged conformal-flow
approach for multiple boundary components [13]. The numerical argument here instead converts
electric flux into a controlled energy decrease and applies the neutral enclosing-area theorem [17,
Theorem 1.3].
   This paper proves the minimum-enclosing-area formulation of the charged spacetime Penrose
conjecture for the exterior data of Definition 2.1. The result uses the invariant ADM mass and
the area in the original metric. It permits nonzero ADM momentum, disconnected weakly future
outer-trapped boundary, both source-free electromagnetic fields, and uncharged non-electromagnetic
matter obeying its dominant energy condition (DEC). Only two metric derivatives and one second-
form derivative are controlled at infinity. Neither a positive area infimum nor a timelike ADM
vector is assumed. Theorem 2.3 establishes both and proves (1.1).
   The equality theorem concerns the entire original initial data. For a connected, outer area-
minimizing, outermost marginal boundary in the nondegenerate range m > Q, equality holds
precisely in the admissible Reissner–Nordström class specified in Theorems 2.5 and 2.6. The original
metric, second form and both electromagnetic fields are recovered by a global spacelike embedding
that is smooth through the future horizon. This includes non-time-symmetric and boosted equality
examples. No statement is made about the region behind the initial boundary.

1.1. Construction of the numerical comparison. The proof first prepares a strict exterior
with compactly supported second form and a smooth rest end. The preparation preserves the two
closed flux forms and compares the original enclosing infima and invariant masses. Its annular
construction uses the stated weak derivatives and integrable constraint sources; it does not impose
parity or higher-order decay on the original data. An energy-raising conformal family then deals
with the possible non-timelike ADM endpoint after the timelike estimate is proved.
   On each prepared exterior we solve a coupled scalar system on an artificial compact filling.
One variable is a graph height; the other controls conformal distortion and compression. A scalar
identity provides positive quadratic terms strong enough to preserve the charged curvature lower
bound after projection of the two flux forms. The system is solved with its floor, boundary-slope
bounds and end normalization, rather than subject to a separate solvability hypothesis.
   Two features of this construction are useful beyond the final inequality. First, the regularity
argument exploits the rank-one axis structure of a uniformly elliptic equation whose axial coefficient
need not have a modulus of continuity. A trace-reversed Hessian flux and a fixed-axis comparison
give gradient control without differentiating that coefficient. Second, the estimates distinguish
constants at a fixed approximation parameter from the quantitative distortion bounds needed
when that parameter tends to infinity. The height separates the filling from a region with a
charged scalar-curvature bound. A second bounded scalar solve converts the total charge into
an exact decrease of ADM energy, while its conformal floor controls every full enclosing area. A
compactly supported pure-trace second form makes a regular height boundary future trapped, so
the neutral theorem applies. Optimizing the flux profile gives both branches of the charged bound.
The Maxwell forms need be closed only on the original exterior; their smooth extensions into the
artificial filling impose no flux constraint there.

1.2. Recovery of equality data. After the numerical inequality, the proof returns to the original
exterior. Variation of the enclosing infimum is performed over its whole compact family of perimeter
minimizers. Constraint separation produces a causal lapse–shift pair. Variations of both closed
flux forms yield exact electromagnetic Killing contractions even when the exterior is not simply
connected. The resulting stationary data are the original data; their possible extra stress is an
aligned null tensor.
4                                                 OPENAI


   The same variation argument excludes larger marginal obstacles with a fixed future or past
sign on each component. This stronger exclusion supplies causal communication with the end. A
smooth bifurcation attachment, including an all-orders normal-jet argument at vanishing lapse,
then places the stationary exterior within the hypotheses of the maximal-hypersurface theorem of
Chruściel and Wald [9]. On that maximal slice, the magnetic potential and momentum identities
adapt the integral staticity method of Sudarsky and Wald [20], with the residual-matter term
treated locally, to force staticity and the disappearance of additional matter. The static orbit
metric has the original invariant mass and boundary area. The complete electrostatic equations
and normalized lapse permit application of the connected-horizon uniqueness theorem of Borghini,
Cederbaum, and Cogo [5, Theorem 3.1]. It identifies the static metric, lapse, and electric potential
with Reissner–Nordström, after which the original slice, horizon embedding and two signed charges
are recovered.

1.3. Organization. Section 2 gives the full hypotheses and conventions. The geometric preliminar-
ies justify positivity and the full-frontier perimeter formulation. The end preparation, filled-system
identity, a priori estimates and elliptic solve construct the comparison data; the charged comparison
closes the numerical proof. The final three sections carry out original-data variation, the stationary
extension and the rigidity and sharpness arguments.


                            2. Initial data and the main theorems
   We use units G = c = 1 and zero cosmological constant. The sign convention for a spacetime
realization is (−, +, +, +) and
                                          K(U, V ) = G(∇U n, V ),
where n is the future unit timelike normal. On an inner boundary, the unit normal ν always points
into the exterior, toward its designated asymptotically Euclidean end. We put H = divS ν and
trS K = (g ij − ν i ν j )Kij . Thus the future outward null expansion is θ+ = H + trS K.

Definition 2.1 (Charged exterior data). Let Ω be a connected oriented smooth three-manifold
with nonempty compact smooth boundary S, and let (g, K, E, B) be smooth up to S. Here g
is Riemannian, K is a symmetric covariant two-tensor, and E, B are vector fields. We require
completeness of the metric space (Ω, g) with its boundary included. Outside a compact set there is
exactly one coordinate end, diffeomorphic to {x ∈ R3 : |x| > R0 }.
   Throughout the proof we use geometric constraint densities:

           2µ = Rg + τ 2 − |K|2g ,                Ji = ∇j (Kij − τ gij ),              τ = trg K,   (2.1)
           µm = µ − (|E|2g + |B|2g ),            Jm = J − 2(E × B)♭ .                               (2.2)

These four densities are 8π times their physical counterparts. The cross product uses the orientation
of Ω. Assume

                  µm ≥ |Jm |g ,         divg E = divg B = 0,       µ, |J|g ∈ L1 (Ω, dVg ).          (2.3)

On the end, for some q > 1/2 and r = |x|, assume

                 gij − δij = O2 (r−q ),       Kij = O1 (r−1−q ),       E i , B i = O1 (r−2 ).       (2.4)

The notation Oj (r−a ) bounds every coordinate derivative of order ℓ ≤ j by Cℓ r−a−ℓ . These are
bounds on otherwise smooth data, not additional higher-derivative decay assumptions.

Here nδ , dAδ are Euclidean, whereas νR , dAg are computed in g. Put Q = (Q2E + Q2B )1/2 .
   No assumption is made about data behind S, and no extension across S is required. In particular,
the single-end assumption concerns this exterior only. Boundary components and compact-interior
topology are unrestricted. There is no assumption of a global electromagnetic vector potential. It
is useful to retain instead the closed two-forms
                                         α1 = ιE dVg ,       α2 = ιB dVg .                          (2.8)
Unless stated otherwise, a change of metric keeps these forms fixed and defines the new vector
fields by contraction with the new volume form. All form norms are exterior-algebra norms.
Definition 2.2 (Full enclosing cut). A full enclosing cut is Γ = ∂D, the entire intrinsic manifold
boundary of a connected smooth codimension-zero submanifold-with-boundary D ⊂ Ω that is
closed in Ω, has manifold interior in int Ω, and contains the whole sufficiently distant part of the end.
The boundary Γ is required to be compact, smooth, embedded and two-sided. It is oriented toward
the end. All components, including any portions coincident with S, are counted. In particular
D = Ω contributes Γ = S. Define, in the original metric,
                                                                       s
                                                                           ag (S)
                                  ag (S) = inf Areag (Γ),       rA =              .                 (2.9)
                                             Γ                              4π
   The closed flux forms have flux 4πQE and 4πQB across every full cut by Stokes’ Theorem.
Opposite charges on different boundary components are allowed. Section 3 proves both positivity
of ag (S) and equivalence with the filled finite-perimeter convention used in the proof; neither a
positive infimum nor an attained smooth minimum is assumed here.
Theorem 2.3 (Charged spacetime Penrose inequality). Let the data satisfy Definition
                                                                                 p 2.1, and

The same conclusion holds if, on each boundary component, one fixes either the condition H +
trS K ≤ 0 or the condition H − trS K ≤ 0.
     The upper-area formulation in (2.10) is essential. It is equivalent to m ≥ Q for 0 < rA ≤ Q, and
to                                                       !
                                  1        Q2

There is no unrestricted polynomial claim below the charge threshold. The theorem also proves
that the possible null ADM endpoint cannot occur with a nonempty compact boundary in this class.
It requires no connectedness, outermostness or area-minimization hypothesis for the numerical
inequality.
Definition 2.4 (Connected horizon class). Data in Theorem 2.3 belong to the connected horizon
class if S is connected, θ+ = 0 on S, every full cut has area at least A = Areag (S) > 0, and there
is no compact smooth embedded two-sided full enclosing surface entirely in int Ω with θ+ ≤ 0
6                                                   OPENAI


everywhere. Such an excluded surface may be disconnected. Thus ag (S) = A. No strictly positive
stability eigenvalue of this marginally outer trapped surface (MOTS) is assumed.
Theorem 2.5 (Original-data equality). Assume the connected horizon class and m > Q. If
                                       s

                                              = m + m2 − Q2 ,                                   (2.12)

then the entire original quadruple (g, K, E, B), including its boundary, is induced by a global smooth
orientation-preserving spacelike embedding into the regular future-horizon extension of one exterior
of dyonic Reissner–Nordström with parameters (m, QE , QB ).
   The image lies in the domain of outer communication together with its corresponding future
horizon. The boundary is a full smooth horizon cross-section or the bifurcation sphere, and the
chosen end approaches the corresponding spatial infinity. The embedding, its future unit normal and
the induced electromagnetic fields extend smoothly to S. The non-electromagnetic matter densities
vanish throughout the exterior.

direct calculation. Theorem 2.5 makes no extremal classification at m = Q and no statement about
data behind the boundary.

                  3. Full enclosing cuts and their minimizing frontiers
  The area in the theorem is an intrinsic exterior invariant. In particular, a portion of a cut
coinciding with the original boundary still contributes area. We establish the compactness and
variation properties of this invariant without using the constraint equations.
  Write ag (S) = Amin (S), with the convention of Definition 2.2. All surface orientations point
toward the end. This normal is the inward normal of the exterior region bounded by the cut.
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                  7

smooth up to S, then for every full enclosing cut Γ and every sufficiently distant coordinate sphere

Proof. Let D be the exterior region defining Γ. Choose r so that Γ lies strictly inside Sr and a
neighborhood of Sr lies in the manifold interior of D. The truncated region Dr is a compact smooth
manifold with intrinsic boundary Γ ⊔ Sr . Its boundary

truncation of Ω proves the other equality. Contact between Γ and S occurs on the smooth intrinsic
boundary of Dr ; it neither deletes a boundary term nor creates another face. Every boundary
component is included.                                                                          □

Proposition 3.2 (Strict positivity of the full-cut area). Under the geometric hypotheses of
Definition 2.1,
                                0 < ag (S) ≤ Areag (S) < ∞.                            (3.2)

and Γ β = 1 for every full cut. Thus ag (S) ≥ Cβ−1 .
    R

Proof. Choose a point of S. Its boundary collar, connectedness of Ω, and the single coordinate
end give an embedded proper ray starting transversely at that point and eventually equal to a
straight coordinate ray. Indeed, join the collar to the end by a path, perturb its compact part to
an embedded arc in dimension three, shorten at its last crossing of a large coordinate sphere, and
attach the outward straight ray. The two joins can be smoothed in disjoint coordinate balls.
   The normal disk bundle of the ray is trivial because its base is an interval. The tubular-
neighborhood construction on its compact portion, followed by the straight tail, gives an embedded
tube
                                       Φ : [0, ∞) × D2 −→ Ω
whose initial disk lies in S, whose positive-parameter part lies in int Ω, and whose tail has a fixed
positive coordinate radius. Let ω Rbe a smooth two-form compactly supported in the interior of the
transverse disk, oriented so that D2 ω = 1. Define β on the tube by Φ∗ β = pr∗D2 ω, and extend by
zero. The transverse compact support makes this extension smooth at the lateral boundary; it is
smooth up to S and closed.
   Its comass is bounded on the compact portion and on the straight tail, the latter because the
coordinate radius is fixed and gij → δij . Thus Cβ = supΩ ∥β∥∗,g < ∞. A sufficiently distant sphere
meets the supporting tail in a positively oriented graph over the entire support of ω and has flux
one. Lemma 3.1 gives the same flux on every full cut. Hence

3.1. An auxiliary perimeter problem. Each component of S is a closed orientable surface
and bounds a compact handlebody. Attach such handlebodies along S, with the appropriate
orientations, and denote their union by F and the resulting boundaryless manifold by Ω.

Lemma 3.3 (Confinement by the end foliation). There are compact truncations CR0 ⊂ int CR1
containing F such that the infimum in Equation 3.3 is attained and every bounded minimizer is
contained in CR0 up to a null set. The same truncations work for a family with uniform asymptotic
bounds gij − δij = O1 (r−q ), q > 0, on a common end. The existence and confinement statements
also hold for an arbitrary bounded measurable obstacle in place of F .

Clipping strictly decreases perimeter if the discarded volume is positive.
   Fix R1 > R0 and clip each member of a minimizing sequence at a good radius in (R0 , R1 ). The
resulting sets lie in CR1 , contain F , and have bounded perimeter and volume. Compactness in
the space BV of functions of bounded variation gives an L1 convergent subsequence, and lower
semicontinuity gives a minimizer. The obstacle condition is closed under L1 convergence. These
compactness and lower-semicontinuity results apply in finitely many coordinate charts on a larger
compact neighborhood [1, Theorem 3.23 and Proposition 3.6]. Equation 3.7 equates this confined
infimum with the unrestricted bounded-set infimum. Applying it to a minimizer at good radii
decreasing to R0 proves that the minimizer has zero volume outside CR0 . Its perimeter support
therefore lies strictly inside CR1 .
   Uniform end bounds make the error in Equation 3.4 uniform, so the same construction works for
the stated family. Only boundedness and the closedness in L1 of the containment condition were
used for the obstacle; a large compact truncation is always a finite-perimeter competitor. This
proves the last assertion.                                                                     □
                               CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                9


Proposition 3.4 (Equivalence with full cuts and obstacle regularity). The auxiliary minimum
equals the intrinsic smooth-cut infimum:
                                               pg (F ) = ag (S).                               (3.8)
Every minimizing set has a regular representative with compact embedded C 1,1 frontier, smooth
and minimal away from the obstacle. Thus its frontier is C 1,α for every 0 < α < 1 and has W 2,2
local graphs. It has no bounded complementary component. Its exterior is connected to the unique
end, and its entire frontier, including contact with S, carries its perimeter measure.
Proof. Let M minimize and let M ′ be a bounded finite-perimeter local competitor, without an
obstacle restriction. The set M ′ ∪ F is admissible. Perimeter submodularity gives
                               Pg (M ) − Pg (M ′ ) ≤ Pg (F ) − Pg (M ′ ∩ F ).                  (3.9)
Choose a smooth compactly supported vector field V with |V |g ≤ 1 which agrees with the outward
unit normal of F on ∂F . The signed-distance collar and a cutoff provide such a field. Gauss–Green
gives                      Z                  Z
                   Pg (F ) =        divg V dVg ,               divg V dVg ≤ Pg (M ′ ∩ F ).
                                F                     M ′ ∩F
Consequently, for Λ = ∥ divg V ∥∞ ,
                     Pg (M ) − Pg (M ′ ) ≤ Λ Volg (F \ M ′ ) ≤ Λ Volg (M △ M ′ ).             (3.10)
The unchanged perimeters outside the variation neighborhood cancel, so this is also a local
inequality.
   The exact obstacle regularity result we use is Huisken–Ilmanen’s Regularity Theorem 1.3(iii): in
a smooth Riemannian domain of ambient dimension less than eight, a pure perimeter minimizer
respecting a C 2 obstacle has C 1,1 boundary, smooth off contact [12]. Here M minimizes against
every compactly supported competitor containing the open obstacle int F ; the metric and obstacle
are smooth, the dimension is three, and the bulk term is zero. Lemma 3.3 keeps its frontier
away from the auxiliary truncation boundary. The regular representative is the closure of its
measure-theoretic interior, whose boundary equals the perimeter support. Away from F , both
signs of every compact normal variation are allowed, so this smooth boundary has mean curvature
zero. The stated Sobolev regularity follows from C 1,1 .
   A compact embedded frontier has finitely many components: it has a finite cover by connected
graph neighborhoods. If its complement had a bounded open component, that component’s
boundary would be a nonempty union of frontier components. Adding it to M removes their
positive area without adding a new frontier and contradicts minimality. There is only one unbounded
complementary component, because b    Ω has one end and M is bounded.
   Given an admissible smooth exterior D with full boundary Γ, the bounded set MD = Ω       b \ int D
contains F and has boundary exactly Γ. Therefore Pg (MD ) = Areag (Γ), including every coincident
portion on S. In particular D = Ω gives MD = F . This proves pg (F ) ≤ ag (S).
   Conversely, flow a minimizing set M for small time s > 0 by a compactly supported smooth
field pointing strictly outward on S. The image Ms contains F in its interior. Indeed it contains
the flowed obstacle, whose boundary in the signed-distance collar has moved strictly outside S.
The flow’s surface Jacobians converge uniformly to one, so Pg (Ms ) → Pg (M ).
   A compact C 1 embedded boundary has a C 1 defining function with nonzero differential near its
boundary. Approximate this function in C 1 by smooth functions; the implicit function theorem
yields smooth embedded boundaries converging in C 1 , with the same enclosed side. Apply this
to ∂Ms . Its positive distance from F preserves enclosure under sufficiently close approximation,
and surface Jacobians give area convergence. Fill bounded complementary components of each
approximation. This deletes components and does not increase area. The closure of the remaining
exterior is connected, contains the whole distant end, and has smooth compact two-sided intrinsic
10                                               OPENAI


boundary in int Ω. It is an admissible full cut. Letting the approximation error and then s tend to
zero proves ag (S) ≤ pg (F ).                                                                    □
Corollary 3.5 (Minimal obstacles). If every component of the smooth obstacle boundary is minimal,
every minimizing frontier in Proposition 3.4 is smooth and minimal everywhere. If a frontier meets
a connected component of the obstacle boundary, it contains that entire component.
Proof. At contact, write frontier and obstacle as graphs u ≥ ψ, with u ∈ C 1,1 and ψ smooth. On
the contact set Du = Dψ and D2 u = D2 ψ almost everywhere. The second statement follows from
the fact that the weak derivative of a Sobolev function vanishes almost everywhere on a level set,
applied to the Lipschitz functions Di (u − ψ). Off contact u solves the minimal graph equation;
at almost every contact point its second derivatives equal those of the minimal graph ψ. Thus
it solves the same equation weakly throughout the patch. The bounded gradient makes this a
uniformly elliptic equation, so interior elliptic regularity and bootstrapping give smoothness. The
strong maximum principle for the difference of the two smooth minimal graph equations gives local
coincidence at each contact point. Contact is also closed, so connectedness extends the coincidence
over the obstacle component.                                                                     □
Corollary 3.6 (Comparison of exterior metrics). If gj ≥ cj g on Ω, cj → 1, and gj → g uniformly
on compact sets, then agj (S) → ag (S).
Proof. Two-dimensional area elements satisfy dAgj ≥ cj dAg , so agj (S) ≥ cj ag (S). For the upper
bound, test with any fixed smooth cut of g-area less than ag (S) + ε, use compact convergence on
that cut, and then let ε ↓ 0.                                                                   □

3.2. Strict compactness and the derivative of the minimum. For a fixed smooth obstacle
F , denote the family of its minimizing filled sets by Mg (F ). Give this family the L1 distance on a
common compact truncation; enlarging that truncation does not change the distance. Tangent
planes are unoriented.
Proposition 3.7 (Strict compactness of minimizing enclosures). Let smooth metrics gj converge

                      1Mj −→ 1M       in L1 ,      Pg (Mj ) −→ Pg (M ) = pg (F ).              (3.11)

Proof. Confinement supplies one compact set C containing every minimizer. Uniform metric

3.3. Compatibility of minimizing sheets. The variation argument averages over the minimizing
family. The next property prevents cancellation between different tangent planes above one spatial
point.
Lemma 3.9 (Compatible planes). For a fixed smooth compact obstacle F , the set
                                                     [
                                            Z=                 ∂M
                                                  M ∈Mg (F )

is compact. At each x ∈ Z, all minimizing frontiers through x have the same unoriented tangent
plane T (x), and T is continuous on Z. If π is a Borel probability measure on Mg (F ) and
                                        Z
                               η(A) =             Areag (A ∩ ∂M ) dπ(M ),
                                        Mg (F )

then, for every continuous function Ψ on the plane bundle,
                    Z      Z                                        Z
                                  Ψ(x, Tx ∂M ) dAg dπ(M ) =             Ψ(x, T (x)) dη(x).      (3.16)
                     Mg (F ) ∂M                                     Z

Proof. If M1 , M2 minimize, their union and intersection contain F . Submodularity gives
              2pg (F ) ≤ Pg (M1 ∪ M2 ) + Pg (M1 ∩ M2 ) ≤ Pg (M1 ) + Pg (M2 ) = 2pg (F ).
Thus both also minimize. If their frontier planes were distinct at an intersection, the tangent
half-spaces would meet transversely. The boundary of their union or intersection would have a
corner: its tangent cone would contain two distinct half-planes. This contradicts its C 1 regularity
from Proposition 3.4.
   Here is the required closedness argument. On the common compact truncation, Equation 3.10
has a fixed constant. It yields uniform density bounds at every frontier point, for sufficiently small
coordinate balls centered there:
                           cr3 ≤ Volg (M ∩ Br ),     cr3 ≤ Volg (Br \ M ),
                                                                                                (3.17)
                                      cr2 ≤ Pg (M ; Br ) ≤ Cr2 .
For clarity, compare with M \ Br and M ∪ Br . For either phase volume v(r), the isoperimetric
inequality for its intersection with the ball and these comparisons give
                         v(r)2/3 ≤ C 2v ′ (r) + Λv(r)
                                                         
                                                               for almost every r.
The coarea formula identifies v ′    with the slice area up to fixed coordinate constants. Since
v(r) ≤ Cr3 , the last term is absorbed for small r. Integrating (v 1/3 )′ ≥ c gives v(r) ≥ cr3 ; both
phases are nonempty at every scale at a perimeter-support point. Relative isoperimetry gives the
perimeter lower bound; the same competitors and the coordinate-sphere area bound give the upper
bound. Smooth compact geometry makes these constants uniform.
   Suppose now Mj → M in Mg (F ) and xj ∈ ∂Mj tend to x. The two volume bounds pass to
every fixed small ball about x under L1 convergence; hence x ∈ ∂M . Huisken–Ilmanen’s Regularity
Theorem 1.3(ii) gives uniform local C 1,α estimates for a fixed 0 < α ≤ 1/2 [12]. Its dependencies
are the distance to the ambient domain boundary, obstacle C 1,α bounds, metric C 1 bounds and
positive definiteness, and the bulk bound, here zero. All are fixed in this application. Arzelà–Ascoli
in local graph charts gives subsequential C 1,β convergence, 0 < β < α. The density bounds and
the L1 limit identify the limiting sheet with ∂M . Thus
                                            Txj ∂Mj −→ Tx ∂M.
The incidence set {(M, x, Tx ∂M ) : M ∈ Mg (F ), x ∈ ∂M } is therefore compact. Its spatial
projection Z is compact, and the previously proved uniqueness of the plane at each limit point
makes T continuous.

   By Proposition 3.7, the surface measures depend weakly continuously on M , so their average
defines the finite Borel measure η. Each surface uses the same plane T (x) at its points. Integrating
this identity proves Equation 3.16.                                                                □

                 4. Charge-preserving preparation of the asymptotic end
   This section constructs the strict rest exteriors used in the comparison argument. The con-
struction preserves the two closed flux forms, controls the area of every full cut, and relates the
resulting energy to the invariant mass of the original end. All constraint densities in this section
are geometric densities, as in Definition 2.1:
                               2µ = Rg + τ 2 − |K|2g ,        J = divg (K − τ g).
Thus the factors in the ADM fluxes remain 16π and 8π.
4.1. Flux forms and conformal changes. Set X1 = E, X2 = B, and αi = ιXi dVg . The
divergence constraints say precisely that dαi = 0. For any other oriented metric h on Ω, the same
forms determine fields by ιXi (h) dVh = αi . Their two fluxes through every full cut remain 4πQE
and 4πQB . This statement uses Stokes’ Theorem in the truncated exterior of the cut, including
each portion coincident with S.
   For |Y |g ≤ 1 define
                                 Ig (Y ) = |X1 |2g + |X2 |2g + 2⟨X1 × X2 , Y ⟩g ,
                                                                                                (4.1)
                                Dg (Y ) = µ + J(Y ) − Ig (Y ) = µm + Jm (Y ).
The inequality 2|X1 × X2 | ≤ |X1 |2 + |X2 |2 gives Ig (Y ) ≥ 0, and
                                           inf Dg (Y ) = µm − |Jm |g .
                                         |Y |g ≤1

Consequently charged DEC implies total DEC. A constant SO(2) rotation of (α1 , α2 ) preserves
both Ig and Dg : it preserves the sum of the squared norms and the cross product.
Lemma 4.1 (Conformal identities). Let u > 0, g ′ = u4 g, K ′ = u2 K, and retain α1 , α2 . Then
                                u4 µ′ = µ − 4u−1 ∆g u,

Proof. The scalar-curvature formula in dimension three is Ru4 g = u−5 (−8∆g u + Rg u). Both τ 2
and |K|2 acquire the factor u−4 . This proves the first identity. Write π = K − τ g and f = log u.
The transformed trace reversal is π ′ = u2 π. In the divergence of this tensor use
                        (∇′A − ∇A )B = 2{df (A)B + df (B)A − g(A, B)∇f }.

give Xi′ = u−6 Xi . In an orthonormal frame their components therefore scale by u−4 , whereas u−2 Y
has the same orthonormal components as Y . The electromagnetic identity and Equation 4.3 follow.
Its last term is nonnegative for u ≥ 1, and K(∇u, Y ) ≥ −|K||du| proves Equation 4.4. Finally
H ′ = u−2 (H + 4∂ν log u) and tr′S K ′ = u−2 trS K.                                               □

Lemma 4.4 (Reduction of the non-timelike endpoint). Suppose the numerical conclusion of

4.4. Compact corrections for the linearized constraints. On Euclidean three-space write
                    (LH) = ∂i ∂j (Hij − tr H δij ),     (Dk)i = ∂j (kij − tr k δij ).
The formal adjoint kernels are respectively the affine functions and the Euclidean Killing fields.
For the scalar operator this follows from ∂i ∂j p − (∆p)δij = 0: taking the trace gives ∆p = 0, then
its Hessian vanishes. For momentum, the adjoint equation is equivalent to the Euclidean Killing
equation after taking its trace. Thus the momentum tests are translations and rotations, with no
dilation test.
Lemma 4.5 (Supported linear inverses). Fix a compact subannulus of {1 < |z| < 4}. A smooth
scalar source f supported there has a compactly supported symmetric solution H of LH = f if its
affine moments vanish. A smooth vector source V has a compactly supported symmetric solution k
of Dk = V if its Killing moments vanish. The solutions can be supported in a fixed larger compact
subannulus, and, for each integer ℓ ≥ 0 and 1 < p < ∞,
                       ∥H∥W ℓ+2,p ≤ C∥f ∥W ℓ,p ,       ∥k∥W ℓ+1,p ≤ C∥V ∥W ℓ,p .                (4.14)
Proof. We first specify the ordinary divergence inverse used in the proof. For a mean-zero smooth
compactly supported source in a ball, the support-preserving Bogovskiı̆ operator gives a smooth
compactly supported vector field, with divergence equal to the source and a gain of one derivative
in W ℓ,p . The regularized integral operators and their support and Sobolev mapping properties
are given in [10, Theorems 3.2 and 4.9, Corollary 4.11]. To use balls on the annulus, cover the
prescribed source support by finitely many balls whose closures lie inside the annulus and join
them by a finite connected family of such balls. Split a source by a partition of unity. Along a
spanning tree of overlapping balls transfer the integral of each leaf piece to its parent using a fixed
unit-integral bump in the overlap. Removing leaves successively leaves each piece with integral
zero, since the total integral is zero. Applying the ball operators and adding the results gives a
divergence inverse with fixed compact support. The finite cutoffs and bumps preserve all the stated
Sobolev estimates. Use successively larger fixed compact supports for successive applications below.
   For a scalar fR with zero
                           R affine moments solve ∂i Vi = f . Compact support and integration
by parts imply Vi = − zi f = 0. Solve ∂j Bij = Vi componentwise. Then the symmetric part
P = (B + B T )/2 satisfies ∂i ∂j Pij = f . In dimension three the inverse of trace reversal is
                               H = P − 12 tr P δ,      H − tr H δ = P.
This gives the scalar solution and gains two derivatives.
18                                                OPENAI


   For momentum, zero translation moments permit a componentwise solve ∂j Bij = Vi . Put
Sij = (Bij − Bji )/2. The rotation moments give
                                         Z                          Z
                                    0=       (zi Vj − zj Vi ) = 2       Sij .

Solve ∂k Dijk = −Sij , choosing D skew in i, j, and define
                                     Cijk = Dijk − Dikj − Djki .
Directly using that skew symmetry gives Cijk = −Cikj and (Cijk − Cjik )/2 = Dijk . It follows that
Pij = Bij + ∂k Cijk is symmetric and ∂j Pij = Vi . Set k = P − 12 tr P δ. There is one net derivative
of gain: the second divergence solve gains a derivative and the displayed correction differentiates it
once. All supports remain compact in the prescribed larger subannulus. Integration against the
adjoint kernels also proves the necessity of the stated moment conditions.                          □

and momentum. For arbitrarily large R there are smooth data (gbR , K cR ) equal to the original data
on r ≤ R and to the reference on r ≥ 4R, such that on the intermediate annulus
                               bR − |JbR |gb ≥ −ηR R−3 ,

and takes values in [0, 1], blend the metric and second form using λ. The linearized constraint error
beyond the blend of the two linear sources is
                                   fR = [L, λ]H,          VR = [D, λ]k.

                 fR = (∂i ∂j λ)P (H)ij + 2(∂i λ)∂j P (H)ij ,            (VR )i = (∂j λ)P (k)ij .       (4.16)

original metric and first derivatives of its second form are used.

   The nonlinear constraint map about (δ, 0) has quadratic remainder bounded in C 0 by CR−2β
on these scaled data. Its sources consequently differ from the convex blend of the original and
vacuum sources by o(R−1 ) + O(R−2β ) in C 0 . The original scaled momentum is O(R−β ); changing
its norm from the original metric to the corrected metric costs only O(R−2β ). Convexity of the
cone {(µ, J) : µ ≥ |J|} therefore proves a scaled DEC deficit of size o(R−1 ) + O(R−2β ) = o(R−1 ).
Undoing the scaling multiplies sources by R−2 and gives Equation 4.15. All corrections are
supported away from the two annular boundaries, so the asserted exact matching and smoothness
follow.                                                                                           □

   Strictification and the limiting comparisons. Fix this repaired exterior and apply the compact-K

                  5. The filled scalar system and its coercive identity
  We work on one fixed strict exterior obtained in Proposition 4.7. Thus K is compactly supported,
the end has smooth asymptotic symbol estimates, and, for some 0 < β < 1 and c > 0,
                               µ + J(Y ) − Ig (Y ) ≥ cr−3−β ,       |Y |g ≤ 1.                   (5.1)
Here and throughout this section the constraint densities are geometric: 2µ = Rg + τ 2 − |K|2 and
J = div(K − τ g), where τ = trg K. The function r agrees with the coordinate radius sufficiently
far out and is smooth and positive elsewhere. We set k = 2; in particular, every transverse block
below has dimension two.
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                   23


5.1. The auxiliary filling and the unknowns. The construction also permits a fixed sign
σi ∈ {1, −1} on each boundary component Si , with HSi + σi trSi K < 0. For the future-trapped
case of Theorem 2.3, all signs are +1. Allowing the signs separately will be useful when another
obstacle is inserted. The signs are fixed during the construction.
Lemma 5.1 (Filling with controlled geometry). For every sufficiently large integer N , the prepared
exterior is contained in a smooth complete, boundaryless, one-ended manifold ΩN . Its metric and
symmetric tensor extend g, K and have the following properties. Each Si has a fixed inner collar
on whose cross-sections H + σi trtan K < 0, with the normal directed toward the exterior. Behind
this collar there is a fixed transition to a product cylinder, a product neck of length O(1 + N ), and
a fixed cap. On the product neck and cap, K = −σi L0 g for a fixed sufficiently large L0 . Local
coordinate bounds for the metric, its inverse, and the tensor, of every fixed derivative order, and a
positive local injectivity radius can be chosen independently of N . The enlarged compact core has
volume O(1 + N ). The extension of r can be chosen uniformly comparable to one there. The closed
flux forms α1 , α2 are retained on the original exterior.
Proof. Every connected component of the boundary is an orientable closed surface, hence is
diffeomorphic to the boundary of a handlebody of its genus. Attach a separate such handlebody on
each component. The collar theorem gives signed collar coordinates, increasing toward the original
exterior. Extend the metric and tensor smoothly a short distance inward. The strict expansion
inequality persists on a shorter collar by continuity.
   On the next fixed collar, interpolate the metric to a product metric. The metrics in this
interpolation are positive definite and form a fixed compact family; their cross-sectional mean
curvatures are therefore bounded. Before making this interpolation, subtract σi Lg from K, with
L ≥ 0 increasing smoothly from zero. This changes σi trtan K by −2L. Start the change within the
region where the expansion is already strict; after L has become large, it dominates the bounded
mean curvature and the bounded tensor terms in the interpolation. One may then interpolate
the remaining tensor to the pure trace −σi L0 g, still retaining strictness. Finish with an exactly
product segment. This gives the asserted fixed transition.
   Choose a smooth metric on each handlebody extending its product boundary collar, and put
K = −σi L0 g on that cap. Insert between the transition and cap as many copies of the fixed
product segment as are needed. A length bounded by a fixed multiple of 1 + N is available, with
the multiple enlarged when the height barriers are chosen. There are finitely many fixed transition
and cap pieces. Product translation identifies all local neck charts, so all asserted local bounds
and the local injectivity radius are uniform. Only the cylinder volume grows with its length. The
original end is unaltered, proving completeness and the one-end assertion. Extending r as a positive
constant along the inserted pieces proves the weight assertion. This construction concerns the
metric and tensor only; all subsequent uses of the flux forms take place on the original exterior,
where they remain closed with their prescribed two fluxes.                                        □
  Fix 0 < ϵ < 1. Choose once and for all a smooth nonincreasing function ϑ : R → [0, 1], equal to
one on (−∞, 0] and zero on [1, ∞). Define, for t ∈ R,

Proof. First obtain the scalar identity before conformal multiplication. On ΩN × R consider the
stationary Lorentzian metric
                             G = −l2 (dz − df )2 + ḡ,       ḡ = g + l2 df 2 .
                                                                          √
Written in the coordinates (x, z), it has spatial metric g, lapse U = l D, and shift γ = l2 ∇f = U w.
Its future unit normal to the constant-z slices is n = U −1 (∂z −γ) = U −1 ∂z −w. With the convention
for K in Definition 2.1, stationarity gives
      b = −U −1 sym ∇γ = −H f − p(dt ⊗ w + w ⊗ dt),            div γ = −U Hb ,   Hb = tr b.      (5.12)
Here sym means the average of a two-tensor and its transpose. The contracted Gauss equation
and normal variation of the second fundamental form give, with this sign convention,
          R = Rg + Hb2 − |b|2 − 2Ric(n, n),         Ric(n, n) = −n(Hb ) − |b|2 + U −1 ∆g U.
Since Hb is independent of z, n(Hb ) = −w(Hb ). Substituting and using div(U w) = −U Hb yields
                            R = Rg + B(b, b) − 2U −1 div(∇U + Hb U w).                           (5.13)
In the coordinates z − f (x) the same metric is the warped static metric −l2 d(z − f )2 + ḡ. Its scalar
curvature is R = Rḡ − 2l−1 ∆ḡ l. The inverse and determinant of ḡ are Ad and D det g, respectively.
Hence
                l−1 ∆ḡ l = U −1 div (U/l)Ad ∇l ,
                                               
                                                     ∇U − (U/l)Ad ∇l = −U b(w, ·).                (5.14)
For the second identity, divide by U and use d log U = p(1 + v)dt + H f (w, ·) and d log l = pdt; the
resulting difference is H f (w, ·) + p{vdt + w(t)w} = −b(w, ·). Equations 5.13 and 5.14 now give
                              1       1      1             −1

  For clarity, the insertion of the original constraint tensor is an exact cancellation. Namely,
                  U −1 div(U PK w) = J(w) + PK : U −1 ∇(U w) = J(w) − B(K, b),

In the block notation, tr T = F − χj, and PQ w has transverse component aw M + pvy and axial

  To check the full expansion, put zT = tr T = F − χj. The transverse, mixed, and axial blocks of

                      1           1   0 2  1 2               2

                                PQ (w, ∇t) = aw ⟨M, y⟩ + pv|y|2 − aw xzT .
Combining the terms in M, y completes the square |M + sp aw y|2 − v|y|2 /4. The remaining linear

5.3. Polynomial coercivity and the stationary-point comparison. We use PN for a positive
bound of the form C(1 + N )a , allowing the constant and the finite exponent to increase between
appearances. Its coefficients may depend on the fixed prepared data and on ϵ, but never on the
outer truncation radius. A bound merely finite for each fixed N will not be denoted by PN .

Lemma 5.5 (The zero-tensor segment). Every smooth zero-Dirichlet solution of the trace equation
with K = 0 has f = 0. On that segment t = Z, d = χ = 1, w = 0, and the second equation becomes
                 2∆g Z + 2(1 + p(Z))|dZ|2 = b{2δ0 sp (Z)|dZ|2 + ρ − m0 (Z)CN ρ0 }.                (5.33)
In particular, at (a, b) = (0, 0) the unique solution is f = Z = 0.
Proof. At a positive interior maximum of f , the left side of Equation 5.31 is nonpositive and
the right side is strictly positive. A negative minimum is excluded in the same way. The zero
boundary value therefore gives f = 0. The defining formulas now give t = Z, T = 2sp |dZ|2 ,
u = eZ l(Z),              Z
        R z sand V = 2e l(Z)∇Z. Differentiating this flux proves Equation 5.33. For b = 0, define
Ψ(z) = 0 e l(s) ds. The equation is ∆g Ψ(Z) = 0 with zero boundary value, so Ψ(Z) = 0 by the
maximum principle. Since Ψ′ > 0, Z = 0. This identifies the starting solution for continuation
without assuming existence at any other parameter value.                                       □

                 6. Height, floor exclusion, and quantitative distortion
  Throughout this section the prepared strict exterior and 0 < ϵ < 1 are fixed. We use the filled
manifolds, coefficients, and equations of Section 5, with dimension three and k = 2. Thus
                                                                        √
              ℓ = e−N ϵ ,     η = ℓ3/2 ,   Z = t + 41 log D,    u = et l D = le2Z−t .
                            CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                  29


All assertions concern smooth solutions on the truncation at r = R, with f = Z = 0 on that sphere.
They apply to the homotopy
                              (a, b) ∈ ([0, 1] × {1}) ∪ ({0} × [0, 1]),
                                                                                               (6.1)
                           Ka = aK,       F = h = ηf,        div V = buΞ,
where all quantities involving the tensor are recomputed using Ka . In particular,
                                                                         η √
          Ξ = δ0 (T + G) + ρ − m0 CN (ρ0 + vρ1 ),        G = ηaw |df |g = ( D − D−1/2 ).     (6.2)
                                                                         l
Here ρ0 = r−3−β , ρ1 = r−2b1 , 3/4 < b1 < 1, ρ = c∗ ρ0 , and m0 = ϑ(N (t + ϵ)).
  Constants denoted by C can depend on the fixed prepared exterior and on ϵ, but not on N , R, or
the homotopy parameters. A symbol PN denotes a positive polynomial in 1 + N with those allowed
dependencies; it can be enlarged at successive occurrences. We will keep polynomial bounds and
bounds of the form exp(PN ) distinct. The enlarged core has volume at most C(1 + N ), uniformly
bounded local geometry, and uniformly bounded Ka and its derivatives. The tensor vanishes
outside that core and a fixed exterior annulus. Radius weights are uniformly comparable to one on
the added necks and caps.

6.1. The height and the outer face.
Lemma 6.1 (Uniform height and outer slope). There are constants C, r0 , independent of N , such
that every smooth solution of the trace equation in (6.1) satisfies
                        |h| ≤ C,      |h| ≤ C(r−b1 − R−b1 )        (r0 ≤ r ≤ R),               (6.3)
provided R ≥ 2r0 . On the outer sphere,
                                        |df |g ≤ Cη −1 R−b1 −1 .                               (6.4)
Consequently, for all R ≥ Rmin (N, ϵ), one has t > −ϵ on that sphere. If a = 0, then f = h = 0.
Proof. At an interior maximum of h, its gradient vanishes, and hence D = 1, Aχ = I. The trace
equation there reads
                                                 l
                                   h = trg Ka + ∆g h ≤ trg Ka .
                                                 η
At an interior minimum the reverse inequality holds. The boundary height is zero and | trg Ka | ≤ C
uniformly on the filled manifolds. This proves the first assertion. It also proves h = 0 when Ka = 0.
   Choose r0 outside the support of K and large enough for the following radial calculation. At a
contact point with HR = C0 (r−b1 − R−b1 ), the slope axis of h is the radial axis. The transverse
eigenvalues of Aχ are one and its axial eigenvalue is 0 < χ ≤ 1. The asymptotic bounds for the
prepared metric imply, uniformly for this entire range of χ,
                      trAχ ∇2 HR = C0 b1 r−b1 −2 −2 + (1 + b1 )χ + o(1) < 0.
                                                                            
                                                                                               (6.5)
Indeed, in the Euclidean metric the radial and transverse Hessian eigenvalues are respectively
C0 b1 (1 + b1 )r−b1 −2 and −C0 b1 r−b1 −2 ; the metric and connection errors are o(r−b1 −2 ). Since
1 − b1 > 0, one choice of r0 makes the inequality strict for every slope and every N . Choose C0 so
that HR ≥ C at r = r0 for every R ≥ 2r0 . If h − HR had a positive interior maximum, the matching
gradients would determine the same t, l, D, and Aχ in evaluating the trace√at that point, while
∇2 h ≤ ∇2 HR . The trace equation, K = 0, and (6.5) would give 0 < h ≤ (l/(η D)) trAχ ∇2 HR < 0.
Applying the same argument to −h proves the end bound. The two barriers vanish at r = R; their
one-sided derivatives there, together with the zero tangential derivative of h, imply (6.4).
   For a fixed boundary slope the function
                                   t 7−→ t + 14 log(1 + l(t)2 |df |2g )
30                                                 OPENAI


has derivative 1 + pv/2 ≥ 1. At t = −ϵ its value is at most

                 −ϵ + 14 log(1 + C 2 ℓ2 η −2 R−2b1 −2 ) = −ϵ + 41 log(1 + C 2 ℓ−1 R−2b1 −2 ).

This is negative for sufficiently large R at fixed N ; for example it suffices to require C 2 ℓ−1 R−2b1 −2 <
e4ϵ − 1. Since its value at the actual boundary t is Z = 0, strict monotonicity gives t > −ϵ.              □

6.2. Exclusion of the lower boundary of the admissible set.

Lemma 6.2 (Floor exclusion). One can choose a fixed δ0 > 0 and a polynomial CN such that no
smooth solution of (6.1) with t ≥ −ϵ touches t = −ϵ, provided R ≥ Rmin (N, ϵ). The choices are
uniform in the homotopy parameters.

Proof. The outer boundary is excluded by Lemma 6.1. Consider first b = 1 and an interior minimum
with t = −ϵ. Write e for the slope axis, arbitrary at zero slope. The stationary-point calculation of
Lemma 5.4 gives
                   1 2t      1                          f           2            2
                        b = 2 Rg − v Ricg (e, e) + S(H ) − vp tre⊥ ∇ t − 2 trAd ∇ t
                   2 e Rg
                          ≤ 21 Rg − v Ricg (e, e) + S(H f ),                                              (6.6)

where S(B) = 12 ((trAd B)2 − |B|2Ad ⊗Ad ). The signs in this inequality use p, v ≥ 0, Ad > 0, and
∇2 t ≥ 0. The same lemma supplies a constant c1 > 0, independent of N , and the algebraic estimate

                                 T − S(H f ) ≥ c1 T − PN (vF 2 + |Ka |2 ).                                (6.7)

Both formulas concern the actual tensor Ka ; no energy condition on the filling or on the scaled
tensor is used.
  By the scalar identity (5.10) and F = ηf , w(F ) = G, so comparison with (6.6) yields

           u−1 div V ≥ T − S(H f ) + G + (µa − Rg /2) + Ja (w) − F trg Ka + v Ricg (e, e)
                       ≥ c1 T + G − BN (ρ0 + vρ1 )

with BN ≤ C(1 + N ), using the coefficient 6N + 25 in Lemma 5.4. To justify the last bound
throughout the noncompact range, observe that µa − Rg /2 = ((tr Ka )2 − |Ka |2 )/2, and that this
term, Ja , Ka , and F tr Ka are supported in the enlarged core, where they are bounded and ρ0 is
bounded below. On the end, |F |2 = |h|2 ≤ Cρ1 by Lemma 6.1, while | Ricg | ≤ Cr−3 ≤ Cρ1 . The
same estimates on a fixed connecting annulus are absorbed into the constant. This proves the
displayed bound with constants independent of R and a.
   At the assumed minimum m0 = 1. Equating the last lower bound with (6.2) gives

                      0 ≥ (c1 − δ0 )T + (1 − δ0 )G + (CN − BN )(ρ0 + vρ1 ) − c∗ ρ0 .                      (6.8)

Choose 0 < δ0 < 21 min(c1 , 1), and then choose the polynomial CN ≥ BN + c∗ + 1. The right side
of (6.8) is positive because ρ0 > 0, a contradiction.
   In the remaining homotopy segment a = 0, Lemma 6.1 gives f = 0. Consequently t = Z, v = 0,
Aχ = I, u = et l(t), and T = (2p + 1)|dt|2 . At an interior minimum t = −ϵ, the equation is

                                          2u∆g t = bu(ρ − CN ρ0 ).

Its left side is nonnegative, whereas its right side is negative if b > 0. If b = 0, set Φ0 (t) = 0t es l(s) ds.
                                                                                                   R

Then ∆g Φ0 (t) = 0 with zero boundary data, hence Φ0 (t) = 0 and t = 0, since Φ0 is strictly increasing.
This proves the assertion on the whole homotopy.                                                              □

6.4. Sobolev and energy estimates on graph patches.
Lemma 6.4 (Sobolev inequality on the unit-warp graph). For each of the core patches just

                Z            1/3            Z

embeddings with the identity of the real line gives an isometric embedding of the ambient product.
The Euclidean mean curvature vector of the graph has norm at most |H1 | + C, because the trace
of the ambient second fundamental form over its three unit tangent vectors is bounded by a fixed
constant.

   The estimates above use only the extended metric and tensor in the filled region. The elec-
tromagnetic flux forms are needed solely on the original exterior, and none has been extended
through a cap. The quantitative conclusions concern height, slope, and distortion; subsequent
elliptic estimates at a fixed N may have arbitrary dependence on that fixed parameter.

                    7. The elliptic construction and its physical end
   We prove the existence assertion needed for the filled comparison. Throughout this section the
dimension is three, so that k = 2. We fix the prepared exterior, ϵ, and then N > max(2, 2/ϵ).
Constants in this section may depend on these fixed choices without a specified rate. The
bounds whose dependence on N matters are precisely those in Proposition 5.3, Lemma 6.2, and
Proposition 6.6. In particular, none of the elliptic constants below is used as a polynomial bound
in N . We use the scalar linear elliptic estimates, maximum principles, and degree theory in [11,
Chapters 6, 8, 9, and 11]. The special estimates for the present coupled system are proved below.
7.1. A scalar estimate with a measurable axial coefficient. The following estimate is the
reason that the trace equation can be treated before the second unknown has a modulus of
continuity. In its statement, the matrix A acts on covectors using the metric.
Lemma 7.1 (The rough axial estimate). Let g range over metrics with uniform ellipticity and
bounded local C 2 norms on a three-dimensional ball. Suppose that z ∈ C 2 , |z| + |dz| ≤ M , and
                                                           ∇z
   A : ∇2 z = G,      A = I − (1 − χ)e ⊗ e,       e=           ,    0 < c ≤ χ ≤ 1,    |G| ≤ M, (7.1)
                                                          |∇z|
where the axis at a zero of dz may be arbitrary. No continuity assumption on χ is imposed. There
are α ∈ (0, 1) and C depending only on the displayed bounds and the patch geometry such that on
every smaller patch                         Z
                            [dz]C α ≤ C,                |∇2 z|2 dVg ≤ Cr1+2α .                    (7.2)
                                               Br (x)
The same assertion holds up to a smooth face on which z is constant; balls in the integral are then
intersected with the domain. It is enough that z be individually C 1 ∩ W 2,s for every finite s and C 2
up to each side of such a reflected face.
Proof. We give the compactness and improvement arguments, including the treatment of the face.
The identities involving third derivatives are distributional. For a C 2 function and metric their
trace-reversed form follows by C 2 smoothing: the fluxes converge locally uniformly, the Hessian
quadratics converge in L1 , and the Ricci terms converge locally uniformly. The original axial
equation is substituted only after this identity has been established; neither χ nor G is differentiated
or mollified. The same argument on each side of a face retains the normal flux jump. Thus the
proof applies in particular to the C 2,α functions arising in continuation below.
   A Hessian estimate above exponent two. Normalize the metric at the center of a small coordinate
ball. The coordinate principal matrix a in Equation 7.1 satisfies
                                           |I − a|F ≤ 1 − c/2
after shrinking the ball by an amount determined by the metric bounds. For a compactly supported
function Y on Euclidean space, the Fourier transform gives ∥D2 Y ∥2 = ∥∆Y ∥2 , where the matrix
norm is Frobenius. The Calderón–Zygmund estimate at any fixed exponent larger than two [11,
Theorem 9.9], interpolated with this identity, implies that the norm of D2 ∆−1 : Ls −→ Ls tends to
one as s ↓ 2. Choose s0 > 2 sufficiently close to two that this norm times 1 − c/2 is less than one.
Writing ∆Y = (I − a) : D2 Y + a : D2 Y and absorbing gives the global estimate. Apply it to ζY ,
and use
