   Iteration and the boundary interface. In the improvement statement b is an allowed upper bound
for the error, and need not equal the actual error. Fix b0 , δ, λ as above, decreasing b0 so that
Cb0 /(1 − λα2 ) < 1/4, and then choose k∗ , ε∗ in Equation 7.7. Initially shrink to a radius R0 and
normalize the gradient by a fixed bound Λ0 so that all errors are at most min(ε∗ , b0 δ). After j drops
38                                               OPENAI


the physical radius and gradient normalization are Rj = R0 r∗j and Λj = Λ0 k∗j . In particular the
normalized forcing is Rj G/Λj , and is multiplied by r∗ /k∗ < 1 at each drop. Metric and connection
errors decrease at least by r∗ and curvature errors by r∗2 . At the first affine entry the physical radius
is Re = r∗ Rj , with the same Λj . Rescale that ball and apply Equation 7.12 with the prescribed
upper bound b = b0 , even if the actual affine error is smaller. On iteration i use bi = b0 λiα2 . The
forcing divided by bi is at most δλi(1−α2 ) ; the same comparison controls the connection errors. The
slope increments are summable by the choice of b0 , so the axis remains separated from zero. Only
constants are subtracted in the rescaled original equation, so its axis is always the actual gradient
axis. These two regimes give uniform affine approximation of order r1+α , including the transition
scale, for any sufficiently small
                                   0 < α ≤ min{α2 , log k∗ / log r∗ }.
The affine approximation criterion for Hölder gradients can also be seen directly: affine slopes at
consecutive dyadic scales differ by Crα , hence converge; comparing overlapping balls gives the same
modulus between two centers. Thus the first estimate in Equation 7.2 follows. Subtract the first
jet in Equation 7.3. Its error is O(r1+α ); connection terms are bounded. Squaring the resulting L2
estimate proves the second estimate.
   For a face use Gaussian coordinates (x′ , x3 ), subtract its constant value, reflect z oddly and
g evenly. The reflected function is C 1 ∩ W 2,s , the metric is Lipschitz, and each smooth side has
bounded curvature. The coordinate Hessian estimate applies unchanged. In the trace-reversed
identity the only additional term is the jump of the normal flux at x3 = 0. Since the tangential
gradient vanishes there,
                               (∇2 z − (∆z)g)∇z · ν = −(∂ν z) trT ∇2 z.
                                                  


The constant boundary value gives | trT ∇2 z| ≤ C|II| |dz|. Thus the jump of the density-weighted
flux is a bounded plane density J(x′ ), of size C|II| |dz|2 . It can be cancelled exactly by adding
the coordinate vector −J(x′ )1{x3 >0} ∂3 to the flux; its divergence is −Jδ{x3 =0} , and no tangential
derivative of J is used. Under a spatial scale Rj and gradient normalization Λj , the normal flux
jump becomes Rj J/Λ2j . Since the current gradient bound is |dz| ≤ Λj , its size is at most CRj |II|,
also after any number of gradient drops. The smooth-side Ricci error is at most CRj2 | Ric |. Both
therefore tend to zero independently of the shrinking gradient normalization. Hence Equation 7.6,
the compactness alternative, and the affine improvement all remain valid across the face. This
proves the boundary assertion.                                                                      □

7.2. Regularity of the two unknowns.
Lemma 7.2 (A small potential estimate). Let M be a symmetric bounded measurable uniformly
elliptic matrix on Br ⊂ R3 . If |B| ≤ C0 and a nonnegative finite measure µ satisfies µ(Bs (x)) ≤
C0 s1+γ for some 0 < γ < 1, then the zero-Dirichlet solution of
                                       − div(M Dv) = div B + µ
satisfies ∥v∥∞ ≤ C(r + rγ ). The assertion applies uniformly to smooth approximations of the data
and hence to their weak limits.
Proof. For the bounded flux part, scale to the unit ball. Testing by (v − k)+ gives {v>k} |Dv|2 ≤
                                                                                          R

C∥B∥2∞ |{v > k}|. Sobolev’s inequality and the level-set iteration give ∥v∥∞ ≤ C∥B∥∞ on the
unit ball; the same argument for −v gives the other sign. Rescaling gives CrC0 . For the measure
part the scalar Dirichlet Green function has the bound 0 ≤ G(x, y) ≤ C|x − y|−1 for bounded
measurable symmetric uniformly elliptic coefficients. This follows from the whole-space bound in
                              CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                  39


[14, Theorem 7.1 and Equation (7.9)] by domain comparison. The coefficients may be extended
elliptically outside the ball; symmetry is preserved. Integrating on dyadic balls centered at x gives
                         Z
                                                     (2−j r)−1 (2−j r)1+γ ≤ Crγ .
                                                 X
                             G(x, y) dµ(y) ≤ C
                                                 j≥0

For completeness the measure belongs to H −1 : its Newton energy is bounded by the displayed
potential bound times µ(Br ), and the Newton energy identity bounds its action on H01 . Mollifications
preserve the mass-growth bound uniformly. Indeed, at scales s < ε their density is bounded by
Cεγ−2 , so their mass is at most Cs3 εγ−2 ≤ Cs1+γ ; at larger scales use the original bound on a ball
enlarged by ε. The bounded divergence-data estimate can first be applied to smooth matrices and
then passed to the weak solution by its energy bound. The measure approximations have uniformly
bounded H −1 norm and uniformly bounded potentials, so weak H01 compactness and weak-star
L∞ compactness give the same estimate for their limit. This proves the lemma.                       □
Proposition 7.3 (Coupled estimates). On a fixed truncation, every smooth solution of the homotopy
in Equation 5.32 satisfying t ≥ −ϵ has local smooth bounds depending only on the fixed data and
N . The bounds hold on uniformly sized interior patches and on outer Dirichlet half-patches,
independently of a sufficiently large truncation radius. In particular there is a fixed exponent α0 > 0
and a uniform local C 1,α0 bound for f and C 0,α0 bound for Z.
Proof. The bounds in Proposition 6.6 give uniform ellipticity of Aχ and boundedness of every
smooth function of x, Z, df that occurs in the equations. The trace equation, divided by lD−1/2 , is
                                              √
                                         2      D               
                                  Aχ : ∇ f =       ηf − trAχ Ka .                           (7.13)
                                                l
Its right side is bounded. Lemma 7.1 therefore bounds df in C α and gives Br |∇2 f |2 ≤ Cr1+2α .
                                                                              R

The exact differential identity in Equation 5.18 gives
                                     |dt| ≤ C(|dZ| + |∇2 f | + 1).                              (7.14)
Write the second equation as
                     div(M dZ + J) = F1 ,         M = 2uAχ ,      J = uAχ Ka (w, ·).
Here M is symmetric uniformly elliptic and J is bounded. The quadratic formula for T and
Equation 7.14 imply
                                  |F1 | ≤ C(1 + |dZ|2 ) + C|∇2 f |2 .                        (7.15)
All volume densities and connection terms can be incorporated into M, J, F1 without changing
these assertions. Set dµ = (1 + |∇2 f |2 ) dx. For a fixed 0 < γ < min(1, 2α) this measure satisfies
µ(Br ) ≤ Cr1+γ .
  Choose a sufficiently large fixed L and put Q± = e±LZ . For either sign the chain rule gives
                  − div(M dQ± ) = div(Q′± J) − Q′± F1 − Q′′± M (dZ, dZ) + J · dZ .
                                                                                       

The range of Z is bounded. Ellipticity and Young’s inequality show that the last term absorbs the
quadratic term in Equation 7.15 if L is large. Thus
                             − div(M dQ± ) ≤ div B± + Cµ,          |B± | ≤ C.                   (7.16)
By Lemma 7.2, subtracting the zero-Dirichlet potential of the right side on Br changes Q± by at
most C(r + rγ ) and makes it a subsolution. The corrected supremum deficit is nonnegative and is
a supersolution. Apply weak Harnack to that deficit. On at least half of Br/2 , either Z is below
the midpoint of its range on Br , or it is above that midpoint. In the first case the Q+ deficit, and
in the second the Q− deficit, is at least a fixed multiple of oscBr Z, up to the potential error. Weak
Harnack then decreases one end of the range on Br/4 by a fixed fraction. More precisely,
                         oscBr/4 Z ≤ θ oscBr Z + C(r + rγ ),         0 < θ < 1.
40                                              OPENAI


Iteration proves a uniform Hölder modulus for Z. At an outer face both unknowns have zero
trace. In flattened coordinates let P = diag(1, 1, −1). After incorporating the volume density,
reflect by Z − = −Z + , M − = P M + P , J − = −P J + , and F1− = −F1+ , where opposite-side values
are evaluated at reflected points. The reflected total flux is −P (M + dZ + + J + ), so its normal
component has matching traces and creates no plane source. For the principal part this also follows
from the vanishing tangential gradient on the constant-data face. In the actual application Ka = 0
near the outer face, hence J = 0 there. The equation and its growth estimate hold across the face,
and the same oscillation argument applies there.
   The coefficients and right side of Equation 7.13 are now Hölder. Interior and Dirichlet Schauder
                     ′
estimates give C 2,α bounds for f , with some α′ > 0. Expanding the second equation, all derivatives
of its coefficients are derivatives of smooth functions of x, Z, df . Consequently it has the form
                    M ij ∂ij Z = H(x, Z, dZ),     |H(x, Z, dZ)| ≤ C(1 + |dZ|2 ),              (7.17)
with a uniformly Hölder principal matrix. No bound for dZ has been used to reach this point.
   Here is the absorption which supplies that bound. For s > 3 the local W 2,s estimate for a Hölder
principal matrix has a uniformly bounded leading constant on sufficiently small patches. On balls
or constant-Dirichlet half-balls the scaled derivative interpolation inequality is
                    ∥dZ∥2L2s (Q) ≤ C oscQ′ Z ∥D2 Z∥Ls (Q′ ) + Cr3/s−2 (oscQ′ Z)2 ,            (7.18)
where Q ⋐ Q′ are concentric patches of comparable radius r. It follows by applying the L∞ –W 2,s
interpolation inequality to a cutoff times Z − c; take c between its maximum and minimum. For
half-balls subtract the zero boundary value and reflect, or use a boundary cutoff in flattened
coordinates. The Hölder modulus makes the oscillation coefficient as small as needed by fixing
a sufficiently small patch radius. To justify absorption locally without any bound depending on
the number of patches, let X be the supremum of their Ls Hessian norms. Each enlarged patch
is covered by a fixed number of the smaller patches. The local estimate and Equation 7.18 give
X ≤ 12 X + C. This supremum is individually finite on every smooth compact truncation. Thus
X ≤ 2C, independently of its radius. Sobolev embedding gives a Hölder gradient for Z. Schauder
estimates applied to both equations now give second derivative bounds; differentiating gives bounds
of every fixed order. The background geometry, end symbols, and outer sphere charts have uniform
bounds at the chosen patch scale. The constants are therefore independent of the sufficiently large
truncation radius.                                                                               □
7.3. The trace solve for arbitrary trials.
Proposition 7.4 (The unrestricted trial trace problem). Fix a smooth compact truncation ΩN,R ,
a ∈ [0, 1], and α ∈ (0, 1). For every Z ∈ C 1,α (ΩN,R ), including trials for which the implicit t is
below −ϵ, there is a unique solution f ∈ C 3,α (ΩN,R ) of
                              trAχ (Ka + H f ) = ηf,      f |∂ΩN,R = 0.
The solution depends continuously on (a, Z) in C 2,α ; bounded sets of trials have uniform bounds
sufficient for this assertion. For a = 0 the solution is identically zero.
Proof. All constants here may depend on a bound for the trial’s C 1,α norm and on this fixed
domain. The global implicit relation in Equation 5.4 makes t, l, D, and Aχ smooth functions of
(x, Z, df ), even at df = 0. The normalized equation is
                                          √                        √
                                2           D                        D
                        Aχ : ∇ f − qf +       trAχ Ka = 0,    q=η      > 0.
                                            l                       l
Continue it from ∆g f = f by using, for σ ∈ [0, 1],
                            √
               2              D
       Aσ : ∇ f − qσ f + σ        trAχ Ka = 0, Aσ = (1 − σ)I + σAχ , qσ = 1 − σ + σq.   (7.19)
                              l
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                  41


The coefficient of f is strictly negative in this convention. At a positive maximum df = 0, so t = Z,
D = 1, and Aχ = I. The maximum principle gives
                                        |f | ≤ H := η −1 ∥ trg K∥∞
for the entire continuation. If K = 0 it gives f = 0.
   We prove a gradient bound before using uniform ellipticity. Write s = |df |. When s → ∞ with
Z in a bounded interval, the implicit equation forces t → −∞. There p = N and l = eN t , and the
defining equation gives
                                                                    √
               log s                   −4/(N +2)           c          D
        t=−          + O(1),      d≍s            ,    χ≥      ,         ≤ C(1 + s).        (7.20)
              N +2                                       1+s         l
These estimates are uniform for bounded trial values; on a bounded s interval they follow from
smoothness and positivity. The lower bound for χ uses N > 2. Notice that it does not use the
floor. The axial eigenvalue χσ = 1 − σ + σχ obeys the same lower bound, while both transverse
eigenvalues equal one. Since |f | ≤ H, Equation 7.19 has normalized source of absolute value at
most C(1 + s).
   Take a short fixed collar of the boundary and a smooth extension of the background metric
across it. Choose a concave increasing modulus
                         ψ(d) = K0−1 log(1 + K0 M d),             ψ ′′ = −K0 (ψ ′ )2 .
First choose K0 sufficiently large depending on the source and geometry bounds. Then choose a
sufficiently short d0 and a sufficiently large M so that ψ ′ ≥ 1 on [0, d0 ] and ψ(d0 ) > 2H. Such
choices are compatible: one can first make K0 d0 < 1/2 and then let M → ∞. For the distance d
to the boundary, the Hessian in its gradient axis is ψ ′′ ; the transverse trace is bounded by Cψ ′ .
Equation 7.20 gives
                                                  (ψ ′ )2
                      χσ ψ ′′ + Cψ ′ ≤ −cK0               + Cψ ′ ≤ (−cK0 /2 + C)ψ ′ .
                                                 1 + ψ′
For large K0 this is strictly more negative than the possible source. The negative barrier has the
opposite strict inequality. At a first contact the gradients agree, and hence all the coefficients
depending on the gradient agree. The strict sign and positivity of qσ therefore exclude contact.
The boundary values and the choice ψ(d0 ) > H give
                             |f (x)| ≤ ψ(dist(x, ∂ΩN,R ))        in the collar.                (7.21)
   For the interior consider f (x) − f (y) − ψ(dist(x, y)) for distances at most d0 , using the short
geodesic distance in the smooth extended metric. The value is nonpositive on the diagonal, at
distance d0 , and whenever one endpoint lies on the boundary, the last assertion by Equation 7.21
and monotonicity of ψ. At a hypothetical positive interior maximum the endpoint gradients are
parallel along the short geodesic and have the same magnitude ψ ′ . Write ex , ey for that common
transported direction. Varying just the first endpoint in its axial direction and then just the second
endpoint gives, respectively,
                            ∇2 fx (ex , ex ) ≤ ψ ′′ ,    ∇2 fy (ey , ey ) ≥ −ψ ′′ .
Paired parallel transverse endpoint variations, summed over the two transverse directions, give
                               tre⊥
                                  x
                                    ∇2 fx − tre⊥
                                               y
                                                 ∇2 fy ≤ C dist(x, y)ψ ′ .
This last bound is the second variation of the short geodesic length; the endpoint Jacobi fields have
equal parallel initial and final values, and their index forms are bounded by curvature times its
length. Thus the difference of the principal operators is at most
                         (χσ (x) + χσ (y))ψ ′′ + Cd0 ψ ′ ≤ (−cK0 + Cd0 )ψ ′ .                  (7.22)
42                                              OPENAI


The right sides of the two equations have difference bounded below by −2C(1 + ψ ′ ). Increasing
K0 gives a contradiction. Crucially, Equation 7.22 uses the two axial coefficients separately; no
modulus of χ(x) − χ(y) is assumed. We conclude |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ ψ(dist(x, y)) for short distances,
and hence |df | ≤ M .
   Uniform ellipticity now follows from this gradient bound. The matrix Aσ still has precisely
the structure of Lemma 7.1, and its right side is bounded. That lemma gives a Hölder gradient.
Schauder estimates then give C 2,β bounds for some β > 0. At this stage df is Lipschitz, and the
prescribed Z is C 1,α , so the coefficients and source of Equation 7.19 are C α . Schauder estimates
give C 2,α bounds. Differentiating the equation and applying the same estimates, with the boundary
estimates for tangential derivatives followed by the equation for the normal second derivative, gives
C 3,α bounds.
   The linearization in f is uniformly elliptic with Hölder coefficients, bounded first-order terms, and
zero-order term −qσ < 0. Derivatives of the gradient-dependent coefficients produce only first-order
terms, because those coefficients do not depend on the value of f . The Dirichlet maximum principle
and linear Schauder solvability make this linearization an isomorphism C02,α → C 0,α . The implicit
function theorem gives openness of the set of continuation parameters. The uniform C 3,α bounds
give compactness in C 2,α and hence closedness. At σ = 0 the unique solution is zero, so the set is
all of [0, 1]. If two solutions of the final equation differed, a positive maximum of their difference
would have matching gradients and an ordered Hessian, while their common q is positive. This
contradicts the equations. Thus the solution is unique. Finally the same implicit function theorem,
now with parameters (a, Z), gives the asserted continuous dependence. Its local branches agree
globally by uniqueness.                                                                                □
7.4. The compact map and the floor.
Theorem 7.5 (Filled solver and normalized end). For the choices in Section 5, Lemma 6.2, and
Proposition 6.6, every sufficiently large N admits a smooth solution (f, Z) of Equation 5.30 on the
complete filled manifold ΩN . It satisfies
                    −ϵ < t ≤ Z ≤ PN ,        ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,       |f | + |df | ≤ exp(PN ).
The height estimates of Lemma 6.1 also hold. On the physical end, for each fixed integer j ≥ 0
there are cj , Cj > 0, allowed to depend on N , such that
                     f = Oj (e−cj r ),    t, Z = Oj (r−1 ),      ∆g t = O(r−3−β ).               (7.23)
In particular both end constants are zero. The solution is obtained by exhausting the outer Dirichlet
radius with N fixed. For the resulting solution uΞ is absolutely integrable, u → 1, and the penalty
vanishes sufficiently far out on the end.
Proof. Fix R ≥ Rmin (N ) as in Lemma 6.1. Parametrize the homotopy by λ ∈ [0, 2]:
                                                (
                                                (1 − λ, 1), 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1,
                               (a(λ), b(λ)) =
                                                (0, 2 − λ), 1 ≤ λ ≤ 2.
All quantities in the trace and scalar identities use Ka = aK; the divergence equation is div V = b uΞ.
Let X = C01,α (ΩN,R ) for a fixed α ∈ (0, 1). For a trial Z ∈ X solve the trace equation by
Proposition 7.4, and evaluate t, w, u, Aχ , T and Ξ at this pair. Define Tλ Z = Y by the linear
zero-Dirichlet equation
                              div(2uAχ dY ) = b uΞ − div(uAχ Ka (w, ·)).                         (7.24)
Only the gradient of the new unknown Y is left unfrozen. On a bounded set of trials, Proposition 7.4
bounds f in C 2,α , and all implicit coefficients have bounded C 1,α norms. The scalar right side
has bounded C 0,α norm, since dt is computed from Equation 5.18. Ellipticity is uniform on that
bounded set by the trial gradient estimate, even when t < −ϵ. Linear Dirichlet Schauder theory
                              CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                      43


gives a uniform C 2,α bound for Y . Thus (λ, Z) 7→ Tλ Z is continuous and compact into X. At a
fixed point the linear solve first gives Z ∈ C 2,α , whereas the trace solution is already f ∈ C 3,α . Thus
Ξ ∈ C 1,α and the principal coefficient and drift flux are C 2,α . Schauder estimates give Z ∈ C 3,α ,
and the trace equation gives f ∈ C 4,α . Repeating gives smoothness. In particular the coupled
a priori estimates apply to individually smooth fixed points; their eventual bounds for all fixed
derivative orders control the chosen X norm, regardless of its initially prescribed exponent α.
   Choose a radius MX larger than the X norm of every above-floor fixed point, which is possible
by Proposition 7.3 and the fixed truncation. Consider the relatively open bounded set

                      U = {(λ, Z) : ∥Z∥X < MX , min t[Z, df [a(λ), Z]] > −ϵ}.                       (7.25)
                                                      ΩN,R

Its openness follows from the continuous dependence in Proposition 7.4. A fixed point cannot occur
at its norm boundary by the choice of MX . At its other boundary it would have minimum t = −ϵ
and would be above the closed floor. Lemma 6.1 excludes such a minimum on the outer boundary;
Lemma 6.2 excludes it in the interior. There is no inner boundary in the filled manifold.
   For completeness the variation of the domain in Equation 7.25 presents no degree obstruction.
The images of the bounded parameter-trial cylinder under T have compact closure. Therefore
any convergent sequence of parameters and associated boundary fixed points would converge to
a boundary fixed point, already excluded. Around any fixed parameter the compact set of its
fixed points has an open neighborhood whose closure lies inside its slice of U. By compactness
the same neighborhood contains every fixed point in the relevant slices for all sufficiently nearby
parameters; otherwise a contrary sequence converges to an excluded fixed point. Excision and the
usual fixed-domain homotopy invariance of Leray–Schauder degree show that the degrees on the
slices are locally constant. A finite interval covering makes them constant along the full path. At
λ = 2 we have Ka = 0, f = 0, and b = 0. The linear solve in Equation 7.24 is therefore identically
zero for every trial. Its sole fixed point is Z = 0, with t = 0 > −ϵ, and the degree of I − T2 on the
terminal slice is one. Hence the initial slice has degree one and contains a fixed point. This proves
existence on every sufficiently large truncation, with the estimates already established.
   We next verify the end before taking the exhaustion limit. All bounds in the remainder of the
proof are uniform in the truncation radius at this fixed N . Outside a fixed sphere K = 0, so
Equation 7.13 is the homogeneous proper equation
                                                               √
                                  2                              D
                         Aχ : ∇ f − q(x)f = 0,        q(x) = η     ≥ η/e > 0.                   (7.26)
                                                                 l
The eigenvalues of Aχ stay in a fixed positive interval. For a sufficiently small fixed c > 0, the
functions Ce−c(r−r0 ) have (Aχ : ∇2 − q)Ce−c(r−r0 ) < 0 for r0 sufficiently large. Indeed their Hessian
is bounded by C(c2 + c/r)e−c(r−r0 ) , whereas the zero-order term is at least (η/e)Ce−c(r−r0 ) . Choose
c first, then r0 . The negatives give the opposite inequality. Choose C to dominate the uniformly
bounded values on r = r0 ; the outer Dirichlet data are zero. Comparison gives |f | ≤ Ce−cr on
every truncated end. The local estimates in Proposition 7.3 give uniform smooth coefficients for
Equation 7.26; its interior and boundary estimates then give exponential decay of every fixed
derivative of f . Alternatively, interpolation between this exponential height bound and the uniform
higher derivative bounds gives the same conclusion, with a possibly smaller positive exponent for
each order. The implicit relation implies that t − Z and its fixed derivatives are exponentially
small.
   On the bounded fixed-N range of Z, define the smooth increasing function Φ by

                                              Φ′′ (z)
             Φ(0) = 0,       Φ′ (0) = 1,              = 1 + p(z) − δ0 sp(z) ,   sp = p + 12 .       (7.27)
                                              Φ′ (z)
44                                             OPENAI


Its derivative is bounded above and away from zero on that range. Putting f = K = 0 in Lemma 5.5
gives exactly
                    2∆g Z + 2 1 + p(Z) − δ0 sp(Z) |dZ|2 = ρ − ϑ(N (Z + ϵ))CN ρ0 .

The exponential bounds just proved and the uniform local derivative bounds show that the actual
end equation differs from this one by Oj (e−cj r ) for every fixed order. Thus
                              |∆g Φ(Z)| ≤ Cr−3−β ,       Φ(Z)|r=R = 0.                          (7.28)
This scalar Poisson bound is independent of the radius R.
  Here are explicit barriers fixing the end constant. Put γ = β/2 and, after increasing r0 , let
W (r) = r−1 − r−1−γ > 0. The prepared metric has g − δ = Oj (r−1 ). Since ∆δ r−1 = 0 and
∆δ r−1−γ = γ(1 + γ)r−3−γ ,
                                        ∆g W ≤ −cγ r−3−γ
for large r0 . Choose a constant multiple of W to dominate the inner boundary values and the right
side of Equation 7.28; this is possible because γ < β. The zero outer boundary values lie between
the positive and negative barriers. The maximum principle therefore gives |Φ(Z)| ≤ Cr−1 , and the
bounds for Φ′ give
                                           |Z| + |t| ≤ Cr−1                                 (7.29)
on every truncated end. In particular no undetermined additive constant is introduced in the
exhaustion.
   We record the derivative decay rather than infer it from an unscaled estimate. Rescale an
annulus of radius r to unit size. The bounded right side of Equation 7.28 and Equation 7.29
give, by local Poisson W 2,s estimates for s > 3, |dΦ(Z)| ≤ Cr−2 and a scaled Hölder bound for
its gradient. The exponentially small error has all unscaled derivatives exponentially small, so it
remains negligible after this rescaling. The other right-side term is a smooth function of Z times
the prescribed weight ρ0 ; its scaled Hölder norms are bounded by Cr−3−β . Schauder estimates
give D2 Z = O(r−3 ). Differentiating the transformed equation and repeating the scaled estimates
inductively gives Z = Oj (r−1 ) for every fixed j. Since t − Z is exponential, the same holds for t.
Finally the untransformed equation gives
                       ∆g t = O(r−3−β ) + O(|dZ|2 ) + O(e−cr ) = O(r−3−β ),
because β < 1 and |dZ|2 = O(r−4 ).
   Take any sequence R → ∞. The compact-patch estimates and a diagonal subsequence give a
smooth solution on ΩN , with all bounds above and the stated end estimates. Initially the limit has
t ≥ −ϵ. If equality held at a point, it would be an interior minimum of a smooth solution and the
same strict contradiction in Lemma 6.2 would apply. Hence t > −ϵ. No limit in N has been taken.
Since Z → 0 and N ϵ > 2, the cutoff ϑ(N (t + ϵ)) vanishes sufficiently far out. The exact zero-trace
expression for T and the exponential trace error give T = O(r−4 ) + O(e−cr ) there. Also ηaw |df | is
exponential, ρ = O(r−3−β ), and u = eZ l(Z) + O(e−cr ) → 1. These functions are integrable over
the three-dimensional end, and smoothness handles the compact part. Thus uΞ ∈ L1 (ΩN ) and the
final assertions follow.                                                                           □

8.2. Separating the filling by height.
Lemma 8.2 (The collar and cap height gap). The product lengths in Lemma 5.1 can be chosen of
order O(1 + N ) so that a number b2 > 0, independent of N , has the following property for all large
N . On each cap, its product neck, and the associated original boundary component,
                                               σi h < −b2 .                                       (8.7)
The inequality also holds on a neighborhood of that boundary component.
Proof. We give the construction when σi = 1. Use the signed collar coordinate s increasing toward
the original end. Choose a small fixed number d0 > 0. On the cap put the comparison height equal
to −d0 . There K = −L0 gN , so the trace of K in Aχ is −L0 (2 + χ) and this constant height is a
supersolution once L0 is large.
   On the adjoining product cylinder choose a nonnegative slope a(s) which starts at zero, becomes
a truncated increasing exponential, and ends at a small fixed positive value a0 . It can be arranged
that                                                 Z
                             ′
                           |a (s)| ≤ C(a(s) + η),       a(s) ds < d0 /8.
Indeed, grow exponentially from a value of order η to a0 , smoothly cutting off near the first value
and making the last value constant. The required length is O(1 + | log η|) = O(1 + N ), and the
integral is bounded by a fixed multiple of a0 . Let H0 (s) be the resulting height, starting at −d0 .
   For a height test H0 , the Hessian tensor in the trace equation is
                                                 ∇2 H0
                                          p                    .
                                              (η/l)2 + |dH0 |2
On a product cylinder only its axial entry can be nonzero. The bound on a′ gives
                                    |a′ |
                               p             ≤ C(1 + l) ≤ C(1 + e).
                                 (η/l)2 + a2
Multiplication by χ ≤ 1 can only improve this estimate. A fixed choice of L0 therefore makes H0 a
supersolution on the entire product, including its small-slope starting portion.
  Continue with a small fixed positive slope through the fixed transition and up to S, keeping
the total increase below d0 /4. On these leaves the prepared extension satisfies Hs + trSs K < 0.
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                      47


Increase the slope within the fixed strict collar on the exterior side until the test height exceeds
the uniform bound C in Equation (8.3) at its outer face. All slopes on these fixed transitions
are bounded below by a fixed positive number, and all their derivatives are fixed. At a putative
comparison contact the gradients of the solution and the test agree, so the solution’s floor gives
η/l ≤ η/ℓ = ℓ1/2 . At such contacts the transverse Hessian trace converges uniformly to Hs , while
χ → 0 uniformly and the axial Hessian contribution vanishes. Thus the full test trace converges
uniformly to Hs + trSs K. First choose d0 smaller than the absolute strict expansion bound on
these fixed collars, and then choose N large. The trace is then below −d0 , hence below the test
height, at every possible contact in the remaining ramp.
   For completeness, the comparison uses the actual solution Z as a fixed function. At a positive
maximum of h − H0 , their gradients agree, so the defining equation for t gives the same t and
the same positive matrix Aχ for both functions. Their Hessians are ordered. Consequently
F [h] ≤ F [H0 ] ≤ H0 < h, contradicting F [h] = h. There is no inner boundary, and the test
dominates on the outer collar face. It follows that h ≤ H0 , which is less than −d0 /2 on the cap,
neck and original boundary. A slightly smaller bound holds on a neighborhood by continuity. For
σi = −1 the same construction applies to −h and −K. There are finitely many components, so
one may take the minimum of their positive height gaps as b2 .                                    □

8.4. Removing the high band by a minimizing enclosure. We shall use the following precise
form of the outermost-horizon existence theorem, also in the rigidity argument.
Lemma 8.5 (Outermost minimal enclosure). Let a smooth connected Riemannian three-manifold
be complete with a nonempty compact smooth minimal boundary included. Suppose it has one end
admitting a foliation by sufficiently large strictly mean-convex spheres, and that the maximum
principle with these spheres confines every compact minimal enclosure to a bounded region. Then
its inner boundary is enclosed by a compact smooth embedded outermost minimal full cut. The cut
may be disconnected and may coincide with components of the given boundary. Its exterior is the
connected component containing the end.
50                                             OPENAI


Proof. Truncate beyond the confining radius and equip the compact region with the auxiliary
second fundamental form zero. The inner boundary has expansion zero with respect to the normal
pointing into the region; the outer sphere has positive expansion with respect to the normal pointing
out. These are the weak inner and strict outer barriers in [2, Theorem 5.1]. In particular neither
strict stability of the inner boundary nor an energy condition is required. Definitions 7.1–7.2 of that
paper prescribe the entire inner boundary in the bounding class of trapped sets. Theorem 7.3 and
Remark 7.4 give the smooth outer boundary of their union and its outermostness. The weak-barrier
version permits coincidence with inner components. Since the auxiliary second form vanishes, the
resulting marginal surface is minimal. Keeping the full frontier of the component incident on the
end, or equivalently filling bounded complementary pockets, gives the asserted full cut. The outer
mean-convex spheres exclude a further compact minimal enclosure outside the truncation. This
proves outermostness in the complete exterior as well as in the compact barrier problem.             □
     Put
                                   HN = {|h| ≥ b3 } ⊂ ΩN .
It is compact by Equation (8.3) and contains all the caps and original boundary components by
Lemma 8.2. Choose
      0 < sN = e−PN < s0 ,      3sN sup |dh|gN < b3 /2,        ON = {distgN (x, HN ) ≤ sN },     (8.23)
                                     ΩN
where s0 is below a uniform background normal-chart radius. Such a choice follows from the
first-derivative bound in Equation (8.3); the polynomial in the exponent may be enlarged. Every
point at distance at most 2sN from ON then lies in the constant plateau |h| ≥ b3 /2. The distance
enlargement does not require b3 to be a regular value of |h|.
Lemma 8.6 (The minimizing frontier avoids the obstacle). For all large N there is a bounded
finite-perimeter set BN containing ON whose full ge-perimeter is minimal among all such bounded
sets. After filling bounded complementary pockets, its entire frontier Γ0N is a nonempty compact
smooth embedded minimal surface, disjoint from ON , and its exterior is connected. Moreover
                                           Pge(BN ) ≤ ePN .                                      (8.24)
Proof. Choose a fixed original coordinate sphere beyond the radius containing {|h| ≥ b3 /4} and
beyond a fixed further neighborhood. It encloses ON for all large N and lies where ψ = 0. Its area
in e2(t+ϵ) (g + l2 df 2 ) is at most ePN by Equation (8.3). This proves the proposed upper bound for
the infimum with a radius independent of N .
   We justify compactness separately at fixed N . Strong asymptotic flatness gives, sufficiently
far out, a foliation by spheres with positive definite outward second fundamental form. Follow
their unit-normal trajectories, parametrized by the radius. Tangential metrics increase along these
trajectories. Projection from an outer leaf to an inner leaf consequently contracts two-dimensional
area. For almost every sufficiently large radius R, clipping a bounded competitor at that leaf does
not increase its perimeter: along each normal trajectory where clipping creates boundary, the
bounded competitor must exit farther out; projection of that portion of its old boundary covers
the new trace, with area counted with multiplicity. BV slicing and the area formula give the
same inequality for finite-perimeter competitors. Thus the infimum over all bounded competitors
equals the infimum in one fixed sufficiently large compact truncation. BV compactness and
lower semicontinuity [1, Theorem 3.23 and Proposition 3.6], and the closed containment condition
B ⊃ ON almost everywhere give a minimizer. Since it minimizes among all bounded competitors,
the outer truncation imposes no local constraint. Large mean-convex spheres also exclude a free
minimal contact with that outer truncation.
   We prove that the perimeter support has no contact with ON . Write
                                            γN = e2(t+ϵ) ḡ.

  Suppose q ∈ ON ∩ supp |D1BN |. Because BN contains ON almost everywhere, such a point is in
∂ON . Take a shortest background geodesic of length sN from q to HN . The ball of radius sN /4
about its midpoint lies in ON ∩ BsN (q). Uniform local background geometry gives
                                          |BN ∩ BsN (q)|gN ≥ cs3N .                               (8.26)
  We obtain a lower bound for the complementary volume without using regularity of either frontier.
Put v(r) = |Br (q) \ BN |gN for 0 < r < sN . Full minimality against BN ∪ Br (q), cancellation outside
52                                                   OPENAI


the ball, and removal of the constant factor e2AN imply, for almost every r,
                            PgN (BN ; Br (q)) ≤ PγN (BN ; Br (q)) ≤ L2N v ′ (r).                   (8.27)
The last derivative is the background area of the complementary trace on the sphere, by coarea.
In the uniform normal charts, background metric balls correspond to Euclidean balls and volume
and perimeter are uniformly comparable. Thus the Euclidean absolute and relative isoperimetric
inequalities [1, Theorem 3.46 and Remark 3.50] hold here with uniform constants. Local absolute
isoperimetry for Br (q) \ BN , including its boundary on the sphere, gives
                       v(r)2/3 ≤ C PgN (BN ; Br (q)) + v ′ (r) ≤ C(L2N + 1)v ′ (r).
                                                                 

Every ball at a point of perimeter support has positive complementary volume. Integrating the
resulting inequality for v 1/3 from δ to r and then letting δ ↓ 0 yields
                                             v(r) ≥ c(L2N + 1)−3 r3 .                              (8.28)
Relative isoperimetry in BsN (q), together with Equations (8.26) and (8.28), therefore forces
                                   PgN (BN ; BsN (q)) ≥ c(L2N + 1)−2 s2N .
Restoring the constant area multiplier in Equation (8.25) gives
                         Pge(BN ) ≥ e2AN c(L2N + 1)−2 s2N ≥ exp(2eN ϵ/4 − PN ).                    (8.29)
For fixed ϵ > 0, this contradicts Equation (8.24) when N is large.
   The minimizing frontier is now disjoint from the obstacle. It is locally perimeter minimizing
without a constraint and is therefore smooth and embedded by three-dimensional perimeter
regularity [12, Regularity Theorem 1.3(iii)]. Filling every bounded complementary pocket cannot
increase perimeter and preserves the obstacle condition. The frontier of the remaining exterior is
the whole smooth minimal boundary incident on the end. It is nonempty because a bounded set
containing the nonempty open obstacle cannot have zero perimeter in the connected filled manifold.
This proves all assertions.                                                                     □

levels 1 + d∗ (1 − 2−j ) proves sup Y ≤ exp(PN ) for a sufficiently large d∗ ≤ exp(PN ). The identical
argument for negative levels bounds inf Y .
   On a fixed truncation, freezing FY and solving the linear uniformly elliptic Dirichlet equation
defines a continuous compact map on the continuous functions with zero boundary values: linear
W 1,p estimates with p > 3 give compactness. The preceding bounds hold for every fixed point of
its homotopy with zero. Leray–Schauder degree therefore gives a fixed point. Divergence-form
local Hölder estimates, then divergence Schauder estimates and differentiation, make it smooth.
The same estimates are uniform on each fixed compact subset as the truncation radius tends to
infinity, so a diagonal limit solves the equation on the filled manifold. These are the standard
uniformly elliptic Dirichlet and interior estimates [11, Chapters 6–8]. Testing by (Y0 − Y )+ gives
zero gradient, since h = 0 on its support and its outer trace vanishes. Consequently Y ≥ Y0 both
on each truncation and in the limit. Inverting B proves y ≥ y0 and, from its exponential growth,
y ≤ PN .

We claim its linear image misses the open cone of functions that are strictly positive everywhere.
   Indeed, a direction in that cone has positive V∗ -coefficient, by its value at infinity. Add its
compact and prototype changes linearly and apply the indicated positive conformal change. The
forms stay closed and retain their end fluxes. On a compact region, strict positivity on the closed
active set makes the first constraint derivative uniformly positive there. Continuity gives this
conclusion in a neighborhood of that set; outside the neighborhood the unperturbed constraint has
a positive minimum. A uniform Taylor remainder therefore gives the DEC for all sufficiently small
positive parameters. The same argument gives the strict chosen boundary expansion.
   Here the far end requires a uniform estimate, rather than compact continuity. The conformal
identities retain a positive multiple of the original nonnegative ball constraint. Their additional
term is a positive constant times tρ∗ . The nonconformal linear errors are tO(r−3−q + r−5 ). The
quadratic metric and tensor prototype errors are O(t2 r−4 ); the remaining conformal and mixed
errors are O(t2 ρ∗ ), after the background nonnegative term has been retained. Since β < q and
β < 1, first choose the end radius large enough for the linear errors to be absorbed, and then choose
t > 0 small enough for the quadratic errors to be absorbed. This proves feasibility simultaneously
60                                               OPENAI


on every end ray. The estimates also show integrability of the changed constraints and their Taylor
remainders. The conformal gradient flux and the explicit prototype fluxes give finite, differentiable
ADM limits. The weak decay, completeness, and cut hypotheses are preserved. Thus these paths
belong to the numerical class; no preservation of equality-subclass outermostness is needed.
   Equation (9.4) and positivity of the third component in Equation (9.10) imply c a′+ (0) > 16πL′ .
This contradicts the numerical bound and Equation (9.2). The claim follows.
   Separation of a linear subspace from a disjoint open convex cone gives a nonzero continuous
functional that annihilates the subspace and is nonnegative on positive functions. The representation
of positive functionals on continuous functions gives positive measures on the three compact test
spaces. If the measure on MB had zero mass, evaluation on V∗ , using Equation (9.8) also at the
adjoined point, would be strictly positive. This contradicts annihilation. Normalize that mass to
one. On the finite active rays divide the resulting measure by ρ∗ , obtaining ΛB . The possible atom
at infinity contributes zero to compact tests and the four prototypes. Rearranging the annihilation
identity proves Equation (9.9) and the weighted finiteness assertion.                               □
9.2. Normal variations and the minimizing sheets. The next step uses a Lorentzian metric
only as a device for specifying finite jets. It does not use existence of a stationary development, or
of an evolution of the additional matter.
Lemma 9.3 (Normal test variation). For each smooth compactly supported function s in the open

                        Dt n = ∇s,         Dt v = v(s)n,        Dt ℓ = ∇s + v(s)n.

At a positive-density active ray, the last term is Jm (∇s) + µm v(s) = 0, and the middle term
is δJm (∇s)/(µm + δ), which tends uniformly to zero. At a zero-density ray both terms vanish.
Together with the proved C 1 convergence and Equation (9.15), this proves uniform convergence
on the compact active set. The variations have no boundary or ADM contribution. Since ΛB is
locally finite, Equation (9.9) and dominated convergence give Equation (9.11).              □
Lemma 9.4. For πB -almost every minimizing set, trTM K = 0 on its sheets in the open exterior
of B.
Proof. There is no cancellation between different tangent planes in Equation (9.11). Indeed,
perimeter submodularity and the common lower bound A show that unions and intersections of
two minimizers are minimizers. Their regularity excludes a transverse crossing. Lemma 3.9 further
shows that the plane incidence set is closed: strict convergence, uniform local density, and graphical
regularity prevent a sheet from disappearing or its tangent plane from changing at a limiting point.
Thus the average of the tangent-plane area measures has the form dη(x) δT (x) , with a single plane
over each relevant spatial point. Equation (9.11) reads
                                       Z
                                           s(x) trT (x) K dη(x) = 0

for every compact smooth s. Hence its integrand vanishes η-almost everywhere. Fubini’s Theorem
gives the claim on πB -almost every frontier. On each free smooth sheet it then holds everywhere
by continuity.                                                                                □
62                                             OPENAI


Proposition 9.5 (Exclusion of larger marginal obstacles). There is no full smooth enlarged
obstacle B of the type described at the start of this section which contains an additional open
subset of Ω outside the original filled obstacle and is marginal with an arbitrary fixed choice of
sign on each  component. For the original obstacle, the area term in Equation (9.9) is exactly
  ′      1R
aS (h) = 2 S trS h dA.
Proof. Every minimizing set for an enlarged obstacle is also a minimizing set for the original
obstacle, because both minima equal A. Choose one of the frontiers supplied by Lemma 9.4.
Away from S this is a free original minimizer, so it is smooth and minimal, including at contact
with an interior component of B. On the coincidence set with that component, its second graph
derivatives agree almost everywhere with those of B. Thus HB = 0 there. Its chosen marginality
gives trTM K = 0, independently of the chosen sign. Off the coincidence set the same assertion
follows from Lemma 9.4. Continuity consequently gives H = trTM K = 0 throughout the frontier
in int Ω.
   At contact with S, the obstacle regularity gives a C 1,α ∩ W 2,2 graph. On its coincidence set
with S the second derivatives agree almost everywhere, and the original condition HS + trS K = 0
applies. Off that set the same equation has just been proved. The frontier therefore satisfies the
uniformly elliptic expansion graph equation almost everywhere through the contact patch. Writing
its principal part in divergence form, local elliptic regularity first gives C 2,α and then smoothness,
since the coefficients and the original data are smooth. The strong comparison principle for two
touching ordered solutions of this graph equation gives local coincidence with S. The contact set
is consequently both open and closed in connected S. If it is nonempty it is all of S, whose area
already equals A; no additional frontier component remains.
   A frontier disjoint from S would instead be a smooth full enclosing surface in the interior with
θ+ = 0, contrary to the original outermostness hypothesis. Thus the chosen frontier must be exactly
S. Its regular bounded filled side is the original obstacle, so it cannot contain the additional open
set of an enlarged obstacle. This proves the exclusion. With B = S, the same reasoning applies to
almost every member selected by πS , showing that their area functionals all equal a′S .            □
9.3. Regularity and normalization of the multipliers. Use the original obstacle from now on,
and suppress its subscript. The scalar and vector first moments of Λ define measures u and X
relative to dVg . Positivity and support on active rays mean, initially as measure statements,
                                 u ≥ |X|g ,     uµm + Jm (X) = 0.                               (9.16)
Moment notation is used here because the measures will immediately be shown to have smooth
densities.
Lemma 9.6. The moments have smooth densities on Ω, with no additional moment mass on S.
In the interior they satisfy
                                            sym ∇X = −uK.                               (9.17)
Their full first jets obey a closed linear first-order system with smooth coefficients.
Proof. For compactly supported interior tensor variations k, the part of C′ depending on k integrates
to                        Z h                                            i
                        2    u(τ tr k − K : k) + X i ∇j (kij − tr k gij ) dV.
There is no interior area term by Proposition 9.5. Integration by parts in distributions and
invertibility of spatial trace reversal give Equation (9.17).
   For a compact interior metric variation h, the second-order principal term is the scalar curvature
adjoint ∇2 u − (∆u)g. Connection variation in the momentum constraint has at most one derivative
of h; after integration by parts it has at most one derivative of u, X. The variations of the fixed
flux forms’ norms and of the transported ray are algebraic. All other terms in the metric equation
                            CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                   63


are therefore smooth linear expressions in u, X, ∇u, ∇X. Taking the trace solves for ∆u, since in
three dimensions tr(∇2 u − (∆u)g) = −2∆u, and hence solves for the full Hessian of u.
   Taking a divergence of Equation (9.17) and using its trace div X = −uτ gives a vector Laplace
equation for X with only first derivatives of the moments on the right. Together with the traced
metric equation this is a linear elliptic system with diagonal Laplace principal symbol. Local
distributional elliptic regularity makes the moment measures smooth. To see that no derivatives
remain unaccounted for, differentiate the symmetrized equation and combine its three index
permutations. For Cij = −2uKij this expresses 2∇i ∇j Xk as ∇i Cjk + ∇j Cik − ∇k Cij plus curvature
contractions with X. Thus every second derivative of X, as well as every second derivative of u, is
a smooth linear function of the first jets. This is the asserted closed system.
   It also proves extension through the boundary. In a smooth collar, restrict the system for the
first jets to each inward normal segment from a fixed interior collar slice. It is a linear ordinary
differential equation with coefficients smooth up to S, so its solution extends smoothly to S. Smooth
dependence on the tangential initial point gives a smooth extension of all the moments. Uniqueness
of the normal equations preserves the identities between these extended jets and derivatives of the
extended fields.
   It remains possible a priori that Λ has a boundary moment not represented by these densities.
Take a metric test with h = ∇h = 0 on S and arbitrary normal second derivatives of its tangential
block. In collar coordinates the principal term Rg′ = ∇i ∇j hij − ∆ tr h − ⟨Ric, h⟩ then has arbitrary
boundary value, through −∂ν2 trS h. The momentum, electromagnetic, transported-ray, area, and
expansion variations vanish on S. After integrating the smooth interior moments by parts, their
boundary terms also vanish because h has zero first jet. Equation (9.9) consequently annihilates
every boundary test against the scalar moment mass. That mass is zero. Its vector moment is
dominated by the scalar mass and is zero as well.                                                   □
Lemma 9.7. For any q0 with 1/2 < q0 < min(q, 1),
                            u = b0 + O2 (r−q0 ),    X i = bi + O2 (r−q0 ).                     (9.18)
Moreover u > 0 in int Ω.
Proof. Write Y = (u, X) in the end coordinates. Inspection of the preceding adjoint and its
prolongation gives
                                |∂ 2 Y | ≤ Cr−1−q0 |∂Y | + Cr−2−q0 |Y |.                       (9.19)
                                                                              2
The derivative coefficients contain ∂g, K; the value coefficients contain ∂ g, ∂K, their quadratic
products, and squared electromagnetic fields. These are controlled by the original O2 /O1 hypotheses;
no extra derivative bound on the original data is being used.
   Here is theR growth argument. On each radial segment, first bound |Y (r)| by its value at a fixed
sphere plus Rr |∂Y (t)|dt. Substitution in the integrated form of Equation (9.19) and Gronwall’s
inequality with the integrable kernel t−1−q0 give uniformly bounded first derivatives and Y = O(r).
The same equation then gives ∂ 2 Y = O(r−1−q0 ), so ∂Y has a limit on each ray with error O(r−q0 ).
Two ray values on a sphere can be joined by an arc of length at most πr; their derivative difference
is O(r−q0 ). Thus the limiting derivative matrix L is independent of the ray, and Y = Lx + O(r1−q0 ).
   The inequality u ≥ |X|g holds in every direction at infinity. Applying it on opposite rays
first forces the linear part of u to vanish, and then forces every linear part of X to vanish.
Consequently ∂Y = O(r−q0 ) and Y = O(r1−q0 ). Reinsert these bounds in Equation (9.19) to
obtain ∂ 2 Y = O(r−1−2q0 ). Its radial integral from infinity gives ∂Y = O(r−2q0 ). Since 2q0 > 1, Y
is bounded. One more insertion gives ∂ 2 Y = O(r−2−q0 ), ∂Y = O(r−1−q0 ), and a common constant
limit Y∞ with error O(r−q0 ).
   Integrate the smooth adjoint identity against the four prototypes, truncated at radius R, and
let R → ∞. The limiting scalar boundary term is 16πu∞ E ′ , and the vector boundary term is
16πX∞  i P ′ . The errors involve a decaying coefficient times a prototype or its derivative, or a
          i
64                                               OPENAI


derivative of Y times a prototype, and their sphere integrals are O(R−q0 ). The volume integrals
converge by the weighted finiteness in Lemma 9.2. The prototypes vanish near S, so Equation (9.9)
identifies these limits with 16π(b0 E ′ + bi Pi′ ). Their four independent flux values give Y∞ = (b0 , bi ),
proving Equation (9.18).
   If u vanished at an interior point, nonnegativity would give du = 0 there and domination would
give X = 0 and ∇X = 0 there. The entire first jet would be zero. Uniqueness along curves for the
closed first-order system in Lemma 9.6 would make u, X identically zero on the connected exterior.
This contradicts u∞ = E/m > 0.                                                                          □

Lemma 9.9 (Global Killing potentials). The spatial one-forms
                           βE = uE ♭ + (B × X)♭ ,       βB = uB ♭ + (X × E)♭                         (9.23)
are globally exact on int Ω. They are the pullbacks of iξ F and iξ (∗G F), respectively. Their potentials
extend smoothly to S.
Proof. Define F uniquely by stationarity and its electric and magnetic contractions with the future
slice normal n = u−1 (∂z − X). With the given orientation its spatial parts are α2 and −α1 for F
and ∗G F. Contraction with ξ = un + X gives Equation (9.23), including both cross-product signs.
   In Equation (9.9), vary α1Rby any smooth compactly supported closed two-form η in the interior.
The only contribution is −4 βE ∧ η, hence it is zero. Varying α2 gives the same assertion for βB .
We explain why this test class implies exactness on arbitrary topology. Testing first with exact
η = dγ proves dβ = 0. For any embedded oriented loop choose a trivialized oriented normal disk
bundle and a compact disk two-form of integral one, extended constantly along the loop and by
zero through the tube sides. It is a compactly supported closed two-form. Since β is closed, its
pairing with this form equals the period of β around the central loop: all parallel loops in the tube
have the same period. Thus every such period is zero. Smooth one-cycles can be represented by
finite sums of embedded loops in a three-manifold, so every period is zero. Path integration defines
a global potential. This argument uses all compact closed two-form variations, not only the exact
ones.
   The forms in Equation (9.23) are smooth up to S. In a collar, integration in the normal direction
extends their potentials smoothly there; local potentials on overlapping charts differ by the same
constants as in the interior, so the extensions agree. Finally, stationarity and Cartan’s identity
give iξ dF = −d(iξ F) = 0 and the corresponding identity for ∗F. Their spatial exterior derivatives
vanish because dα1 = dα2 = 0. These two conclusions give dF = d(∗F) = 0 on the product.            □

in the remaining compact interior region because u > 0 there. Extension by zero proves the last
claim.                                                                                        □

       10. The communicating stationary region and a maximal hypersurface
  Throughout this section the hypotheses of the connected, nondegenerate equality case hold. We
use the fields furnished by Propositions 9.8 and 9.10. Put
                            R = Rz × int Ω,        ξ = ∂z ,     W = u2 − |X|2g .
Thus
              G = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz) = −W dz 2 + 2X ♭ dz + g.   (10.1)
Here and below products of one-forms in metric formulas are symmetric products; in particular,
dU dV has components GU V = GV U = 1/2. The smooth two-form F and the metric are invariant
under the complete flow of ξ. The vector ξ is future causal and nonzero on R, since u > 0 there
and u ≥ |X|g . Also
                                     G−1 (dz, dz) = −u−2 < 0.                            (10.2)
Consequently z is a temporal function even where W = 0. Near infinity and on a deleted collar of
S we have W > 0, and the equations there are the source-free Einstein–Maxwell equations. The
constant κ > 0 is the one in Proposition 9.10.
                            CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                   67


10.1. Communication with the end. On the original stationary end chart, write ρ = |x| for the
Euclidean spatial radius.
Proposition 10.1. Let E = R × {ρ > R}, with R sufficiently large. Taking chronological past and
future within R, one has
                                   I − (E) = R = I + (E).
Proof. Write A = I − (E); the future case has the same proof with time orientation reversed. The
end has timelike Killing orbits, so E ⊂ A. The set A is open and invariant under every Killing
translation. It is connected: a point of A can be joined to E by a timelike curve entirely in A, and
E is connected. Hence A = R × A for a connected open subset A of int Ω containing its distant end.
   Suppose that its relative boundary H = ∂R A is nonempty. A boundary of a chronological
past is achronal and locally separates its past side from an excluded future side. To recall the
local regularity used here, choose a small coordinate cylinder whose vertical lines are timelike.
Achronicity permits at most one boundary point on each such line. The openness of the past
set and local timelike cone bounds show that the boundary is a graph over a spatial ball, with a
uniform Lipschitz bound. Shrinking the cylinder removes any edge of this graph. This description
also shows that both of its local sides are nonempty.
   The boundary is invariant. At every differentiability point its tangent hyperplane therefore
contains ξ. An achronal tangent hyperplane cannot contain a timelike vector. Since ξ is nonzero
and causal, it is null there, and the hyperplane is precisely ξ ⊥ . Continuity gives W = 0 throughout
H. This proves smoothness as well. Indeed, in the preceding graph chart write the graph as
x0 = f (x1 , x2 , x3 ). The smooth plane field ξ ⊥ is transverse to the vertical direction. Its graph
slopes are smooth functions ai (x0 , x); the weak derivatives of f satisfy ∂i f = ai (f (x), x) almost
everywhere. The right sides are continuous. Mollification, or integration on coordinate lines, shows
that these are the classical derivatives of a C 1 function. This identity then gives successively
C 2 , C 3 , . . . regularity. In particular no regular-level-set assumption on W is involved.
   Intersecting with z = 0 gives a smooth embedded two-sided surface C = ∂A in int Ω. The
intersection is transverse because the z-slice is spacelike while the null normal ξ is nonzero.
Invariance identifies H with R × C. Since W > 0 in a deleted collar of S and on the end, C lies
in a compact subset of int Ω. It is closed there and thus compact. A compact smooth embedded
surface has finitely many components: finitely many connected local submanifold charts cover it,
and each component contains one of these charts. In particular there is no accumulating family of
extra frontier components.
   Choose the normal νC into the end-side exterior. On each connected component of C,
                                 ξ = u(n + ενC ),      ε ∈ {1, −1},
where n is the future normal to z = 0 and the sign is constant on that component. The null second
form of H along its Killing normal is zero: for tangent vectors Y, Z to a spacelike section it is
symmetric by hypersurface orthogonality and has symmetric part 12 (Lξ G)(Y, Z) = 0. Its trace
therefore vanishes. Thus C is marginal for one of the two expansion signs on each component.
   The connected collar of the connected surface S contains no frontier point. It is consequently
wholly contained in A or wholly excluded from A. The closure of A in Ω is a connected closed
smooth exterior with full boundary C ∪ S in the first case and C in the second. It contains the
distant end. The local excluded side at every point of C shows that its complementary obstacle
contains an additional nonempty open subset outside the original obstacle. Its components have the
marginal signs just determined, together with the original sign on S if that component is present.
This contradicts Proposition 9.5. Hence H is empty. Connectedness of R proves the assertion. □
   The componentwise choice of sign in Proposition 9.5 is essential here: a boundary of a chrono-
logical future need not have the future marginal sign of the original surface.

10.2. A smooth bifurcation attachment. We first prove the regularity fact needed when the
stationary lapse vanishes at the original boundary. All covariant derivatives in the next lemma are
spacetime covariant derivatives taken on the open collar. Their limiting coefficients are evaluated
in a spatial orthonormal frame and the future normal of the original slices.
Lemma 10.2. Suppose u|S = 0. The tensors ∇j F and ∇j Rm, for every integer j ≥ 0, have
smooth one-sided limiting coefficients at S in this frame. So does ∇ξ. At S the latter tensor is the
endomorphism B given by
                               Bν = κn,           Bn = κν,             B|T S = 0.               (10.3)
Every limiting curvature and Maxwell jet is invariant under B, acting on all its tensor and derivative
slots. It is also invariant under the map Ib which reverses both n and ν and fixes T S.
Proof. Choose a smooth spatial orthonormal frame ei on an original collar patch and extend it,
and n, by stationarity. For spatial directions the spacetime connection is nonsingular:
                             ∇ei n = Ki j ej ,           ∇ei ej = Γkij ek + Kij n.
In particular spatial covariant differentiation preserves smooth one-sided coefficients. The spatial
derivatives of ξ = un + X are
                          ∇ei ξ = (ei u + K(ei , X))n + (uKi j + Di X j )ej .                   (10.4)
Skew symmetry of ∇ξ determines its remaining components without division by u. The boundary
identities u = 0, X = 0, DX = 0, and du = κν ♭ give Equation (10.3). Also [ξ, ei ] = [ξ, n] = 0,
so the connection coefficients along ξ are the coefficients of ∇ei ξ and ∇n ξ and are bounded and
smooth in the same sense.
  The order-zero Maxwell coefficients are the original electric and magnetic fields. Gauss–Codazzi
determines Rijkl and Rnijk from g, K and their spatial derivatives. In the deleted collar the Ricci

then supplies the remaining curvature block (with the same curvature convention in Gauss–Codazzi
and this identity). All order-zero coefficients therefore have smooth limits.
   Here is an induction which controls every normal derivative. Write Fj = ∇j F and Rj = ∇j Rm,
with derivative slots listed from outermost to innermost. Suppose all orders below j have smooth
limits. Any component of order j having a spatial derivative slot has a smooth limit: commute
that derivative to the outermost slot and use the displayed spatial connection formulas. The
commutators are sums of contractions of lower derivatives of curvature with lower derivatives of
the tensor being differentiated. Their total derivative order is j − 2, so the induction hypothesis
controls every term.
   It remains to treat derivative slots n, . . . , n. Apply j − 1 covariant derivatives to closure and
co-closure of F, and evaluate their free derivative slots on n. Metric compatibility allows these
tensor identities to be contracted directly; derivatives of a chosen extension of the frame do not

  The differentiated differential Bianchi identity similarly gives
                    Rj (nj ; i, l, a, b) = −Rj (nj−1 , i; l, n, a, b) − Rj (nj−1 , l; n, i, a, b).     (10.8)
By pair symmetry this covers every curvature component except the block with two normal tensor
slots, Rj (nj ; n, i, n, l). Differentiating Equation (10.5) supplies that component. The differentiated
Ricci tensor is a sum of products of the already controlled F0 , . . . , Fj . This closes the joint induction.
Each right-hand side has smooth coefficients on the original closed collar, so the conclusion is
smooth one-sided dependence, including all spatial derivatives, rather than merely bounded limits.
No identity in the induction solves a time evolution equation by dividing by u.
   For T = ∇j F or ∇j Rm, Killing invariance of the connection gives Lξ T = 0. Written covariantly
this is
                                              0 = ∇ξ T + (∇ξ) · T.
The first term tends to zero: it is u∇n T +X i ∇ei T , and the next-order jets have just been controlled.
Thus B · T = 0 at the edge. In the null basis n + ν, n − ν the two eigenvalues of B are κ, −κ. A
component of an invariant covariant tensor can be nonzero only when its total boost weight is zero.
Counting its derivative slots as well as its tensor slots, it therefore contains equal numbers of the
two normal null directions. Their simultaneous sign reversal has even total degree. This proves the
Ib invariance.                                                                                         □
Lemma 10.3. Let a(r, y) be smooth for r ≥ 0, with y in a compact smooth manifold. If all odd
r-derivatives vanish at r = 0, then a(r, y) = a0 (r2 , y) for a function a0 smooth up to r2 = 0. If all
even derivatives vanish, then a(r, y) = ra1 (r2 , y) with a1 smooth. Both assertions hold for tensor
coefficients in smooth local frames and uniformly with all tangential derivatives.

where bL is smooth. Put p = r2 . Repeatedly applying ∂p = (2r)−1 ∂r to the remainder, up to order
h ≤ L, bounds the result by CpL+1−h ; the same estimate holds after any prescribed tangential
                      √
derivatives. Hence a( p, y) has the derivatives at p = 0 prescribed by the polynomial, continuously
to every order. In the second case, Hadamard’s formula writes a = rb with b smooth; the Taylor
coefficients of b have the parity of the first case. This proof also treats a smooth flat remainder
and makes no analyticity assumption.                                                             □

The one-form ιξ F annihilates ξ. On the orthogonal space of a null vector the metric is positive
semidefinite with precisely its null line as kernel. The last equality therefore implies that e
is proportional to the metric dual of ξ, hence to dW , at the horizon. Smooth division gives
e = b dW + W γ with b, γ smooth on the collar. The potentially singular terms become
                                      b                         U
                      dz ∧ b dW = − dU ∧ dV,         dz ∧ W γ = dV ∧ γ.
                                      κ                         κ
All remaining terms are smooth pullbacks from (p, y).
   Now suppose u|S = 0. In original normal distance s the boundary identities give u = κs + O(s2 )
and X = O(s2 ) with smooth Taylor factors. Thus the tensors
                             As = W −1 X ♭ ,           hs = g + W −1 X ♭ ⊗ X ♭                 (10.11)
are smooth up to S, and hs is positive definite and equals g there. The metric is G = −W (dz −
As )2 + hs . Let (r, y) be the Gaussian normal coordinates of hs at S, and put
                                      Z r
                          H(r, y) =         (As )r (t, y) dt,   z ′ = z − H(r, y).
                                       0
In these coordinates As − dH has no radial component. It follows that Grr = 1 and Gra = 0 for
a ̸= r. Therefore the curves r 7→ (z ′ , r, y) are unit spacelike geodesics, and YA = ∂yA and ξ = ∂z ′
along them are Jacobi fields. Notice that r is a smooth original defining function with r = s + O(s2 ).
   We prove the precise parity of their coefficients. The connection along these geodesics is smooth
one-sided: their velocities are smooth linear combinations of original spatial directions and ξ,
whose connection coefficients were controlled in Lemma 10.2. A parallel orthonormal frame with a
prescribed limiting basis at r = 0 is therefore obtained by a linear ODE with smooth coefficients
on a closed half interval. The radial velocity has limiting value ν. The values YA (0) are tangent to
S, while ξ(0) = 0 and Dr ξ(0) = κn.
   For completeness, Dr YA (0) is normal to S. The Killing identity ∇(∇ξ) = Rm ∗ ξ shows that
the tangential derivative of B = ∇ξ vanishes at the edge. Hence the projector P = B 2 /κ2 onto
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                    71


span(n, ν) is parallel there in every tangent direction. The vector (1 − P )∂r vanishes at the edge,
with its tangential derivatives. Differentiating this equality tangentially yields (1 − P )∇YA ∂r = 0.
Since coordinate fields commute, this is the assertion about Dr YA (0).
  Let Ib act in the parallel frame as in Lemma 10.2. The jth radial derivative of curvature at
zero is its jth covariant derivative contracted with j copies of ν. Its transformation under Ib
consequently acquires the factor (−1)j . Differentiate the Jacobi equation
                                        Dr2 Y + R(Y, ∂r )∂r = 0.
The initial values are fixed by Ib and their initial derivatives change sign. Induction in this equation
gives Ib Drj Y (0) = (−1)j Drj Y (0) for both Y = YA and Y = ξ. The contractions G(YA , YB ), G(ξ, YA )
and G(ξ, ξ) therefore have only even Taylor coefficients. The initial values and Equation (10.3)
give
                            G(ξ, YA ) = O(r2 ),     G(ξ, ξ) = −κ2 r2 + O(r4 ).
For F, the same differentiated contraction rule uses all its covariant jets. Contractions on two
Jacobi fields have even parity, and contractions with one radial velocity and one Jacobi field have
odd parity. Also F(ξ, YA ) = O(r2 ) because ξ(0) = 0. Lemma 10.3 now gives the following actual
smooth coefficient formulas, with p = r2 :
                G = dr2 + (−κ2 p + p2 a) dz ′2 + 2pbA dz ′ dy A + cAB dy A dy B ,               (10.12)
                F = 21 fAB dy A ∧ dy B + peA dz ′ ∧ dy A + rqA dr ∧ dy A + rd dr ∧ dz ′ .       (10.13)
All coefficients in these displays are smooth in (p, y) up to p = 0. Smooth dependence of the
geodesic and parallel-frame ODEs on y makes these conclusions uniform with every tangential
derivative. The statements are tensorial on overlapping charts of S, so they define global tensors
on its collar.
  Set

Equations (10.12)–(10.13) are thus smooth in (U, V, y). Their metric at U = V = 0 has normal
block dU dV and tangential block g|T S .
   In each case extend the finitely many smooth coefficient tensors in p ≥ 0 to |p| < p0 , keeping the
displayed invariant forms. A smooth extension exists by collar extension in each coordinate patch
and a partition of unity on the compact S. Lorentz signature and time orientation hold near B0
by the nondegenerate leading blocks. They hold on the entire sufficiently p     small band: the boost
       −κt           κt
U 7→ e U , V 7→ e V takes any point with p ̸= 0 to one with |U | = |V | = |p|, and takes a point
on a punctured null face arbitrarily close to B0 . The extended formulas are invariant under these
boosts.
   Glue this open band to the deleted collar of R by Equation (10.9) or Equation (10.14). This is
a collar gluing of smooth manifolds. To check its separation property, a sequence in the overlap
which tends to an added null face has p → 0 or unbounded original time and cannot have a limit
in R; a sequence tending to the outer collar boundary has no limit in the open added band. No
pair of distinct retained points is therefore identified by a limiting overlap sequence. Local collar
charts and the old charts give a Hausdorff, second-countable manifold. The translations and boosts
agree on the overlap, and both are defined for every real parameter. Their resulting Killing flow is
complete.
72                                              OPENAI


   In the positive-lapse case the original regular chart gives U = 0, V = eκz on S; in the zero-lapse
case r = 0 gives B0 and the original z = 0 slice has a smooth finite z ′ offset. These observations
also identify the induced metric on B0 and the indicated original boundary. The extension was
constructed for this geometric purpose; only its restriction to R is used in the field equations
below.                                                                                             □
Remark 10.5. The tensors in Equation (10.11) will also be useful after obtaining staticity. In the
positive-lapse case they satisfy
                          1                              dW 2    1
