Thus hs extends as a smooth positive tensor to the collar with defining function r = W , and
is invariant under r 7→ −r as a tensor. Its coefficients with one radial index have odd parity;
                                                                                            √ the
others have even parity. In the zero-lapse case As is smooth in the original structure, and W is a
smooth positive multiple of the original defining function. The two smooth collar structures need
not coincide in the positive-lapse case.
10.3. The stationary end and a global temporal graph.
Proposition 10.6. The stationary end admits coordinates (t, x1 , x2 , x3 ) with ξ = ∂t . Writing
ρ = |x| in this chart, one has for every fixed integer j ≥ 0
                               Gab − ηab = Oj (ρ−1 ),     Fab = Oj (ρ−2 ).                      (10.15)
All these estimates have the corresponding scaled Hölder bounds. The coordinates are obtained from
a constant rest chart by stationary corrections O2,γ (ρ1−q1 ), where 1/2 < q1 < q0 < min(q, 1) and
0 < γ < 1 can be chosen smaller as needed. In particular, on the end the orbit metric hs satisfies
hs − δ = Oj (ρ−1 ) and W = 1 + Oj (ρ−1 ).
Proof. Proposition 9.8 gives a constant limiting metric for Equation (10.1), with error O2 (ρ−q0 ),
and a unit timelike limiting ξ, since (b0 )2 − |b|2 = 1. A constant linear change of coordinates
puts this metric in rest form η while keeping the new spatial coordinates constant along Killing
orbits. Explicitly, in the limiting product coordinates one can take t∗ = z − bi xi and a spatial linear
map whose Euclidean quadratic form is δij + bi bj . The old and new spatial radii are comparable.
Maxwell components remain O1 (ρ−2 ) under this constant change.
   We describe the coordinate improvement to account for the weak initial derivative bounds. For
a stationary scalar the wave operator has the form
                 L = Gij ∂i ∂j + di ∂i ,   Gij = δ ij + O1 (ρ−q0 ),   di = O0 (ρ−1−q0 ).
It is uniformly elliptic on a sufficiently distant end because W > 0 there. Solve Lχa = −□ xa∗ , for
the three linear spatial rest coordinates and for x0∗ = t∗ . The sources are O0,γ (ρ−1−q1 ); this Hölder
estimate follows on scaled annuli from the available second metric derivatives, after decreasing q1
and, if necessary, γ.
   Here is a construction of the right inverse at this weight. Extend the scalar operator to the
Euclidean Laplacian inside a large sphere with a smooth cutoff. This extension is only for the
coordinate equation. For a source f = O0,γ (ρ−1−q1 ) the Newton kernel with its constant term
subtracted on the far tail defines
                                         1          1       χ∞ (y)
                                            Z                     
                            P f (x) = −                 −            f (y) dy,
                                        4π R3 |x − y|         |y|
where χ∞ = 1 outside a fixed ball and vanishes near zero. The tail is absolutely convergent
because the difference of kernels is O(|x| |y|−2 ). Splitting the integral into |y| < |x|/2, |y| ≍ |x|,
and R|y| > 2|x| gives |P f (x)| ≤ C(1 + |x|)1−q1 . For example, the last region is bounded by
       ∞ −1−q1
C|x| 2|x| s      ds; the other regions give the same power. Differentiation of the kernel for first
                            CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                   73


derivatives and scaled interior Schauder estimates for second derivatives give the weighted C 2,γ
bound. Thus P is a bounded right inverse from weight −1 − q1 to weight 1 − q1 .
  After rescaling the cutoff radius to one, the coefficient norm of L − ∆ between these spaces tends
to zero with that radius. The convergent Neumann series for 1 + P (L − ∆) gives the required
corrections χa = O2,γ (ρ1−q1 ). They are smooth by local elliptic regularity. Their first derivatives
tend to zero, so the new spatial coordinates are a diffeomorphism on a further tail. Since the
corrections are stationary,
                            □ t = □ xi = 0,     ξ(t) = 1,     ξ(xi ) = 0.
In particular ξ remains exactly ∂t .
   In these harmonic coordinates the reduced Ricci equation has scalar principal part
                               Gij ∂i ∂j Gab = Qab (G, ∂G) − 2Ricab ,                         (10.16)
where Q is quadratic in first derivatives with smooth bounded coefficients near η. The coordinate
construction starts with scaled C 1,γ bounds for the metric error and scaled C 0,γ bounds for the
Maxwell field. The Ricci source is quadratic in F, since the end is electrovacuum. Schauder
estimates in Equation (10.16) therefore give scaled C 2,γ bounds for the metric. The Maxwell
equations then give one additional field derivative. One way to see the latter step precisely is to
square closure and co-closure:
                                          □ F = Rm ∗ F.
By stationarity this is elliptic. Its coefficients have the metric regularity just obtained and its
zeroth-order source has scaled C 0,γ bounds. Interior W 2,p estimates first improve the bounded first
                          ′
field derivatives to C 0,γ bounds for any fixed γ ′ < 1 by taking p large; Schauder estimates then
apply. Equivalently the stationary Hodge system is elliptic because the symbol of d + δ squares to
Gij ζi ζj > 0 for a nonzero spatial covector. Repeating these two estimates gives, on every scaled
annulus,
                            ∂ j (G − η) = O(ρ−q1 −j ),   ∂ j F = O(ρ−2−j )
for every fixed j. The constants may depend on j. This bootstrap uses only the original two metric
derivatives and one field derivative to start, and only ellipticity on the distant timelike region.
   It follows from Equation (10.16), replacing its principal coefficients by δ ij and using the just-
proved second derivative estimate, that
                                ∆δ (Gab − ηab ) = Oj (ρ−2−2q1 + ρ−4 )                         (10.17)
for every j. The right side is integrable in three dimensions because 2q1 > 1. Multiply a component
of the decaying perturbation by a cutoff on the end and call its Euclidean Laplacian f . The
unsubtracted Newton integral of f is now O(ρ−1 ): the inner region is bounded by Cρ−1 ∥f ∥L1
and the comparable and outer regions are O(ρ−2q1 ). Its difference from the cutoff perturbation is
an entire harmonic function tending to zero, hence vanishes by the maximum principle. Scaled
estimates for the Poisson equation and its derivatives prove the first estimate in Equation (10.15)
to every order. The field estimate is already known. One further derivative implies each desired
scaled Hölder estimate. Finally hs,ij = Gij − G0i G0j /G00 and W = −G00 give the asserted orbit
estimates.                                                                                       □
Lemma 10.7. There is a smooth function s0 on R satisfying ξ(s0 ) = 1, whose differential is
timelike, such that
                                                1     V
                                          s0 =    log                                 (10.18)
                                               2κ     U
near the attachment and s0 = t + constant on the harmonic rest end of Proposition 10.6. Each
level of s0 is a Cauchy hypersurface of R.
74                                               OPENAI


Proof. The covector in Equation (10.18) is timelike on a sufficiently small positive collar. This can
                        √
be checked at U = V = p in the smooth metrics of Proposition 10.4 and then everywhere on the
positive collar by boost invariance. In the positive-lapse case it is dz − (2κW )−1 dW . Choose a
smooth radial cutoff χ(W ) equal to one near zero and zero outside this collar and set
                                                1 W χ(w)
                                                    Z
                                     s0 = z −               dw.
                                               2κ        w
The integration constant is chosen so that the displayed local formula holds exactly. Its differential
is a convex combination of dz and dz − (2κW )−1 dW . Both covectors have value one on the future
causal ξ, so they lie in the same component of the timelike covector cone; that cone is convex. This
proves timelikeness and extends s0 to z plus a constant off the collar.
   In the zero-lapse case Equation (10.18) is z ′ = z − H(r, y). Cut off H in a sufficiently thin
original collar. The additional spatial differential stays bounded because H = O(r) and a cutoff
derivative of order r−1 is multiplied by H. For any bounded spatial one-form λ,
                            G−1 (dz + λ, dz + λ) = |λ|2g − u−2 (1 − λ(X))2 .
Here u = κr + O(r2 ) and X = O(r2 ). Thus the negative term dominates uniformly on a sufficiently
thin collar, including the cutoff annulus. This again continues the desired time as z plus a constant.
   On the far end replace this time by the harmonic rest time t plus a constant. Both time
covectors have value one on ξ; their limiting Minkowski covectors, and the segment joining them,
are uniformly timelike. Their difference of functions is a spatial linear function plus O(ρ1−q1 ) and a
constant. If the interpolation cutoff varies by one over a sufficiently long interval of log ρ, the extra
differential is at most C/L, where L is the length of that interval. Choose L large, and then its
starting radius large. This error is smaller than the uniform timelike margin of the convex segment.
The resulting smooth function has all the asserted local formulas and is temporal globally. Each
modification was a globally defined stationary function, so ξ(s√0 ) = 1 is preserved.
   We check the Cauchy assertion quantitatively. Write r = U V in the positive collar and use
(s0 , r, y) there. At s0 = 0 the smooth extension has a positive radial coefficient, a positive tangential
metric, and time coefficient a negative constant times r2 . The remaining terms in the formulas of
Proposition 10.4 can be absorbed into these leading quadratic forms for small r. Uniformly for a
causal tangent vector one obtains
                                       |dr|2 + |dy|2 ≤ Cr2 |ds0 |2 .                              (10.19)
Here |dy| is measured in a fixed metric on the compact S. Boost invariance extends the bound to
every s0 . In particular |d log(U V )/ds0 | ≤ C, so a causal curve cannot reach the deleted inner end
in a finite amount of s0 time. On the outer end, Equation (10.15) gives |dx/ds0 | ≤ C, excluding
escape to spatial infinity in finite time. On the compact part between these two regions, temporality
and stationary smooth coefficients give the same bounded-speed estimate in any fixed auxiliary
spatial metric. This argument also covers compact loci where W = 0, since u and ds0 remain
nondegenerate there.
   If an inextendible future causal curve had a finite upper endpoint for its s0 range, these estimates
would confine it to a compact spatial set away from both ends and make its spatial coordinates
Cauchy as s0 approaches that endpoint. It would have a limit in R and could be extended by a
short timelike curve, a contradiction. The past argument is identical. Thus its s0 range is all of R,
and strict monotonicity gives exactly one intersection with every level.                             □
10.4. Causality of the attachment and its domain of dependence. Let
                                       Σ = {s0 = 0} ∪ B0 ⊂ R.
                                                           e                                      (10.20)
Near B0 it is parametrized by U = V = r ≥ 0 and y ∈ S. It is therefore a smooth spacelike
hypersurface with boundary B0 . It is closed: the local parametrization includes its only limits on
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                     75


the added faces, while escaping the asymptotically Euclidean end has no limit in the spacetime. It
is connected and has a compact core and one Euclidean end. Its induced metric is complete with
the boundary included.
Lemma 10.8. After decreasing the width of the added band, the full spacetime R
                                                                             e is strongly causal
and Σ is acausal. Moreover
                                        int D e
                                             R
                                                (Σ) = R.                                  (10.21)
The equality holds using either inextendible causal curves or the timelike-curve convention for
domains of dependence.
Proof. We retain only a sufficiently small open band |U V | < p0 on the added side. The invariant
smooth metric formulas imply
             GU U = U 2 a(p, y),   GV V = V 2 b(p, y),     GU V = c(p, y),     c ≥ c0 > 0,
where a, b, c are smooth and bounded for |p| < p0 . Choose a fixed large C and define
                                    U = U e−Cp ,         V = V e−Cp .                            (10.22)
A direct computation gives
                       e2Cp |dU|2G = U 2 {a(1 − Cp)2 − 2C(1 − Cp)c + C 2 p2 b}.                  (10.23)
The analogous formula interchanges U, a with V, b. Taking C large and then p0 small makes the
braces strictly negative. The two gradients are nonzero even on their respective null faces. With
the right-wedge future orientation, U is nonincreasing and V nondecreasing along future causal
curves in the band. Their difference T = V − U has timelike differential: the two causal covectors
have opposite orientations and are linearly independent. In particular T is temporal through the
intersection of the faces.
   These monotonicities also control curves which leave a coordinate neighborhood. First the right
wedge is causally convex in the full attachment. A future curve leaving it across a null face must
cross U = 0 into U ≤ 0, V > 0. It cannot return across that face because U cannot increase to
positive values. It cannot leave the added band elsewhere except through the right wedge, the only
old region continuing beyond the band. The corresponding past statement uses V = 0. The same
sign argument excludes a departure and return through the right wedge for endpoints in any one
of the other open quadrants. Thus the temporal functions s0 and T establish strong causality at
points off the faces.
   There is one additional global issue at a face: a curve could otherwise travel far into the right
wedge and return near that face. Fix a small p1 > 0 at which the exact logarithmic formula (10.18)
still holds. Suppose a future causal curve with endpoints a, b sufficiently close to a face leaves p < p1
through e, and subsequently returns through f . Both crossing points have p = p1 in the right
wedge. The segment between them has nondecreasing s0 , by causal convexity and Lemma 10.7.
Hence
                                 Vf     Ve
                                     ≥     ,     Vf ≥ Ve , Uf ≤ Ue .
                                 Uf     Ue
Monotonicity on the initial and final band segments then gives
                               U(a)V(b) ≥ U(e)V(f ) ≥ p1 e−2Cp1 > 0.                             (10.24)
If either factor has the wrong sign, such a curve is already impossible. Otherwise the left side tends
to zero as both endpoints approach any fixed point on a null face, contradicting the fixed positive
lower bound. This excludes the excursion.
   For a curve that stays in the band, both corrected coordinates stay between their endpoint
values. The map (U, V ) 7→ (U, V) is a local diffeomorphism throughout the smaller band, since its
Jacobian is e−2Cp (1 − 2Cp) > 0. Thus endpoints near a fixed face point confine U, V to a fixed
76                                               OPENAI


small coordinate box. In that box and for all y ∈ S, compactness and the timelike covector dT
give a uniform causal speed bound |dy| ≤ C dT . A sufficiently small difference of endpoint T
values prevents angular escape from any prescribed neighborhood of the point. Combined with
Equation (10.24), this is the defining local no-departure-and-return property of strong causality at
every face point, including B0 .
   The open part of Σ is acausal by its temporal level-set description and causal convexity of the
right wedge. No causal curve can join it to B0 . A future curve from B0 starts with U ≤ 0, V ≥ 0
and cannot enter the right wedge; a past curve has the opposite obstruction from V ≤ 0. A
hypothetical connection in the other direction is the same statement with endpoints reversed. A
causal curve between two points of B0 would have both corrected coordinates constant zero. It
would then be tangent to B0 , whose metric is positive definite, and must be constant. Hence the
closed hypersurface Σ is acausal.
   Every inextendible causal curve in the full spacetime through a point of the right wedge has a
maximal portion in that wedge which is inextendible as a curve of the wedge. By Lemma 10.7 this
portion intersects s0 = 0. Thus R ⊂ D(Σ), and openness gives the inclusion into its interior.
   For the reverse inclusion, in the left wedge U, V < 0 the complete Killing orbits are timelike and
avoid Σ. In the future quadrant U < 0, V > 0, a timelike curve can be continued to the past into
           p while staying in the band. To verify this directly, boost a point to (U, V ) = (−d, d),
the left wedge
where d = |U V |. At fixed y follow the past-directed segment
                      U (λ) = −d + 12 dλ,       V (λ) = d − 2dλ,     0 ≤ λ ≤ 1.
Its leading normal metric norm is a strictly negative constant times d2 , and the perturbation
is smaller uniformly when the band is small. It is therefore timelike. Along it |U V | ≤ d2 < p0 ,
and it ends in the left wedge. Continue there on a complete timelike Killing orbit in the past
direction; in the future direction remain in the future quadrant until an inextendible end of the
curve. Both portions avoid Σ. The past quadrant has the time-reversed construction. Corners can
be rounded inside the open timelike cones. Thus every point of the three other open quadrants lies
on an inextendible timelike curve avoiding Σ. None belongs to D(Σ) even for the timelike-curve
convention. Finally each null face has empty interior and borders an excluded quadrant. It cannot
contribute to int D(Σ). This proves Equation (10.21) with either convention.                    □

  Let Mext denote the Killing development of this end, as in their definition of black and white

                  ∂ j (G − η) = O(ε⟨y⟩−1−j ),      ε = O(R−1 ),       ⟨y⟩ = 1 + |y|,
with the same scaled Hölder bounds. This auxiliary extension is used only to solve the end graph

                                  |a|

                              log(2 + |y|)
and the corresponding scaled Hölder seminorm, with a(0) = 0. The Euclidean Newton operator

                 0,γ
inverse from C−2     to this space. Indeed, splitting the integral into |z| < |y|/2, |z| ≍ |y|, and
|z| > 2|y| gives the logarithmic bound for its value and O(⟨y⟩−1 ) for its first derivative; local
scaled Schauder estimates give the second derivative and its seminorm. The subtracted kernel is
O(|y||z|−2 ) in the last region, so that integral converges absolutely.
   On a ball of radius Cε in this norm, Q(0) = O0,γ (ε⟨y⟩−2 ) and the Lipschitz norm of Q into
  0,γ
C−2 is O(ε). These estimates follow by writing the principal coefficients as their Euclidean values
plus O(G − η) + O(|Da|2 ); all remaining terms contain a metric derivative. The contraction map
a 7→ −P Q(a) therefore gives the required graph. Differentiating its uniformly elliptic equation
on scaled annuli gives all higher derivatives. Returning to x and cutting off the graph on a fixed
annulus gives a smooth spacelike reference hypersurface Σe equal to Σ∗ on the compact core, and
maximal on the distant end, where its height ae satisfies
                           ae = O(log ρ),       ∂ j ae = O(ρ−j )   (j ≥ 1).
The cutoff is spacelike because its gradient is small on that annulus. The end Killing time τe = t−ae
still has G − η = Oj (ρ−1 ) in its coordinates.
   Subtract the same cut-off end correction from τ0 and call the resulting time function τ . Its
differential remains timelike: it is unchanged on the compact core, and the correction has uniformly
small gradient in the transition annulus and the end. Thus τ is smooth through B0 , has Σe as
its zero hypersurface in D(Σ), and equals τe on the distant end. Its zero level is Cauchy in R:
the compact and inner ends are unchanged, and the end correction is sublinear with vanishing
derivative, so the end causal speed bound and no-escape argument, or [9, Lemma 4.2], apply.
Exhaust Σe by its compact truncations, whose boundaries are B0 ∪ Ci with Ci an end coordinate
sphere. Compactness of their domains of dependence is supplied by [9, Theorem 3.2], using the
communication condition already verified. It is this compactness, not merely compactness of the
truncations, that permits the smooth Dirichlet construction of [3, Theorem 4.2]. As in the cited
proof, its domain and edge conditions hold for these acausal truncations. We obtain smooth
maximal spans Mi with the exact boundary B0 ∪ Ci .
   For clarity, the uniform height argument permits this fixed inner boundary. Choose a fixed
sufficiently distant cylinder ρ = R0 , and translate Mi by a Killing parameter bi so that its least τ
value on the cylinder is 1. Spacelikeness bounds the oscillation on that cylinder uniformly. The
compactness statement [9, Corollary 3.6] then confines the translated compact portions to one
compact set. The geometric constants in Bartnik’s tilt estimates are uniform on these surfaces:
on the compact portion this follows from the smooth regular time τ , and on the end from its
stationary identification with τe and the strong coefficient bounds. In particular the curvature,
the lapse and its first logarithmic derivative, and the derivative of the reference unit normal are
bounded. If bi ≤ 0, the translated outer boundary has time bi , while B0 is fixed and has time
zero. Comparison on the end with the maximal reference translates also bounds the translated
height above: its interface height is bounded, and its outer height is nonpositive. Together with
                             CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                     79


the compact-core bound this gives a uniform upper bound for the translated height. Thus the
cylinder is separated by at least one unit of time from both boundary values, and its distance
below the supremum of the translated height is uniformly bounded. The interior tilt estimate [3,
Theorem 3.1(iv), Equation (3.14)] gives a uniform tilt bound there. The exterior test-function
argument in [3, proof of Theorem 5.3, Equations (5.13)–(5.18)] now bounds −bi . That argument
integrates only over the end, after subtracting an exterior barrier and the outer boundary time. Its
cylinder trace has bounded oscillation and bounded tilt; these are precisely the two inner-boundary
estimates used in its boundary term. The fixed surface B0 is outside this integration region and
contributes no additional term. The test function gives log(2 + |bi |) ≤ C, with C independent
of i. For large positive bi , an additional bounded translation puts the greatest cylinder time at
−1; applying the same argument with time reversed bounds bi . This is the translation argument
in [9, proof of Theorem 4.2, Equations (4.22)–(4.26)]. Consequently |bi | is uniformly bounded.
Compactness on the interior and comparison with the maximal reference translates on the end give
|τ | ≤ C on the original Mi .
    The ensuing tilt estimate includes the actual boundary. Both components of ∂Mi lie in {τ = 0};
their ambient mean-curvature vectors have a uniform bound, since B0 is fixed and smooth and Ci
are end spheres. The boundary maximum argument in [3, Theorem 3.1(iii), Equations (3.10)–(3.12)]
therefore gives a uniform tilt bound on all of Mi , including B0 . Its hypotheses bound the geometry
of the prescribed edge; they do not assume the unknown boundary gradient. In smooth coordinates
across B0 this makes the graph equation uniformly elliptic with fixed smooth boundary data.
Boundary gradient Hölder estimates and Schauder estimates, followed by bootstrapping, give
uniform bounds of every order on each fixed closed collar. The interior estimates give the same
conclusion on every compact subset. A diagonal subsequence consequently converges smoothly, up
to the same embedded B0 , to a spacelike maximal hypersurface.
    One can also read off its end estimates directly. Relative to Σe , the graphs Mi have uniformly
bounded heights. For any fixed 0 < β < 1, the graphs ae ± Cρ−β are upper and lower barriers
outside a fixed large sphere. The principal term has the strict sign of ∆ρ−β = β(β − 1)ρ−β−2 ; the
coefficient and drift errors are lower order, since Dae = O(ρ−1 ), D2 ae = O(ρ−2 ) and the stationary
metric has the strong decay above. Choose C to dominate the bounded inner trace. The outer trace
relative to Σe is zero, so comparison gives the same bound uniformly in i. Scaled elliptic estimates
give all derivative and Hölder bounds for the limiting difference. Thus its full end graph has
f = O(log ρ) and ∂ j f = O(ρ−j ) for j ≥ 1, which imply (10.25) for every q2 < 1. In particular its
induced metric has two controlled asymptotic derivatives and its second form has one, as required
below. The Cauchy assertion and covering translates follow from [9, Lemma 4.2 and Theorem 4.2].
    Finally, the complete nonzero causal orbit of ξ through any point of R is an inextendible causal
curve there, so it meets the Cauchy hypersurface exactly once. It is transverse to that hypersurface:
a nonzero causal vector cannot be tangent to a spacelike hyperplane. The intersection map is
smooth by the implicit function theorem, giving the stated global graph and covering property
directly. Its future lapse is strictly positive in the interior. This application uses the existence part
of the cited theorem; the additional alternatives stated there for a foliation are not needed.         □
Lemma 10.10. The Killing graph over Σ     b respects the whole original orbit space. On its boundary
collar√the projection is a smooth diffeomorphism when the orbit space is given the defining function
r = U V . The global electric and magnetic Killing potentials from Lemma 9.9 pull back to exact
smooth potentials on b Σ, smooth up to B0 and constant there.
Proof. Only the boundary assertion requires more than the global graph already proved. In a
smooth collar of Σb choose a defining function q ≥ 0. Its boundary is the actual embedded surface
B0 , and its inward spacelike tangent has a positive spacelike component in the (U, V ) normal plane.
Because its interior is in U, V > 0, the two components of this normal-plane vector are both strictly
positive. Subtracting its component tangent to B0 does not change that conclusion, since the
80                                             OPENAI


normal and tangential blocks are orthogonal at B0 . Hadamard’s formula consequently gives
                   U = q a(q, y),     V = q b(q, y),     a(0, y) > 0, b(0, y) > 0.
                      √
It follows that r = q ab is a smooth defining function, and the orbit projection (q, y) 7→ (r, y)
has an invertible boundary derivative. Its boundary map on S is the identity in these natural
labels. Also the graph time s0 = (2κ)−1 log(b/a)√is smooth to the boundary. This proves the collar
diffeomorphism. In the positive-lapse case r = W ; it is not the original defining function W . In
the zero-lapse case it is the Gaussian coordinate of Equation (10.14).
   The Killing contractions ιξ F and ιξ (∗G F) are invariant and annihilate ξ. Their pullbacks under
any two sections of the orbit projection are therefore the same one-forms on the orbit space.
Their global exactness on the original interior implies exactness on Σ   b \ B0 . Both contractions
are smooth on the attachment and vanish on B0 , since ξ = 0 there. Their restrictions to the
closed hypersurface are closed by continuity from its interior. Local collar primitives consequently
extend the global primitives smoothly: the difference of two primitives has zero differential on an
overlapping connected interior patch and is a constant. A finite collar cover, or integration along
its radial curves, matches these constants. The resulting boundary differential is zero, and B0 is
connected, so each potential has one constant value on the whole boundary. This reasoning uses
no simple-connectivity assumption on Ω.                                                           □

               11. Staticity, recovery of the original data, and sharpness
     Throughout the rigidity argument the hypotheses of Theorem 2.5 hold. Set
                                           q

We use the stationary metric G, Maxwell form F, and Killing field ξ = ∂z constructed in
Proposition 9.8. In particular, their restriction to the original section z = 0 induces the original
four tensors, with the prescribed future normal and orientation. The constructions of Section 10
have not yet asserted staticity or removed the possible residual matter stress. Both conclusions
will follow from an integral on the maximal hypersurface.
11.1. A global maximal graph and the staticity identity. The maximal-slice integral strategy
below adapts the staticity method of Sudarsky and Wald; compare [20, Eq. (40) and Theorem 2].
We derive the identity locally, including the duality rotation, global magnetic-potential argument
and aligned residual-matter term. These features and the present hypotheses are not supplied by
the cited electrovacuum staticity theorem.
Lemma 11.1 (The maximal hypersurface as an orbit section). Let Σ′ be the maximal hypersurface
of Proposition 10.9, and let B0 be its bifurcation boundary. Projection along the Killing flow
identifies int Σ′ diffeomorphically with int Ω. This identification takes the end to the same end and
identifies the boundary labels on B0 with those on S. Compact cuts contained in the interiors of
the two hypersurfaces, with corresponding orbit labels, are homologous in the right wedge.
Proof. Each Killing orbit in the right wedge is a complete, nonzero causal curve. It is inextendible
there: in the original product its spatial label is fixed and its z coordinate traverses all of R. The
Cauchy property of int Σ′ therefore gives exactly one intersection with each orbit. A nonzero
causal vector cannot be tangent to a spacelike hypersurface. Consequently the restriction of orbit
projection is a local diffeomorphism, and its bijectivity makes it a global diffeomorphism. Flowing
the section thus gives a diffeomorphism
                                      R × int Σ′ −→ R × int Ω.
For a compact cut its two sections bound the compact chain swept out by the intervening finite
orbit segments, which proves the homology assertion without a filling behind either boundary.
                                CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                            81


   For clarity, the identification of boundary labels does not assert that the two hypersurfaces
have the same transverse defining function. In the attachment coordinates of Proposition 10.4,
B0 = {U = V = 0} and the right wedge has U, V > 0. A spacelike hypersurface through B0 is
transverse to both null faces. If s ≥ 0 is a smooth defining function on that hypersurface, then
                        U = s a(s, y),         V = s b(s, y),       a(0, y), b(0, y) > 0.
      √            √
Thus U V = s ab is a smooth defining function, and the restriction of y to B0 is its original
boundary label. This also proves that the graph approaches precisely that boundary and no other
limiting orbit. The end assertion follows from the rest-chart graph estimates in Proposition 10.9. □

11.2. The mass and boundary of the static orbit metric. On the open orbit space, the now
positive function W gives
                                   g ′ = hs = g + W −1 Xg♭ ⊗ Xg♭ ≥ g.                            (11.7)
This is an equality of metrics after orbit projection in the interior; different boundary defining
functions are allowed. The induced metric of B0 equals the original metric on T S by the attachment.
Thus |B0 |g′ = A. The fixed set B0 is totally geodesic in spacetime: its second fundamental vector
is fixed by the differential of every Killing flow, while that differential acts as a nontrivial boost
in the normal two-plane and has no fixed vector there. In particular B0 is minimal in g ′ . The
constraint and static equations give
                            Rg′ = 2|E ′ |2g′ ,        divg′ E ′ = 0,        ∆g′ u′ = |E ′ |2g′ u′ .          (11.8)
For the last equation, the Ricci component in the future unit normal direction of a static metric is
(u′ )−1 ∆g′ u′ . Electrovacuum has trace-free stress and that component of its Ricci tensor is |E ′ |2 .

Lemma 11.3 (The original invariant mass is the static ADM energy). The metric g ′ is complete

                        ′

Proposition 10.9 and Lemma 11.1. The symbol bounds for the stationary end in Proposition 10.6,
together with Equation (11.7), give the stated strong asymptotic flatness and field bounds. Fluxes
were checked in the proof of Proposition 11.2.
   We spell out why the mass is that of the original data. In the unimproved stationary coordinates
the spacetime perturbation of its limiting constant Minkowski metric has differentiated decay
O2 (r−q0 ), q0 > 1/2. It is electrovacuum near infinity. The skew-slot flux in Lemma 4.2 therefore
has spacetime divergence
                                            O(r−2−2q0 ) + O(r−4 ).

O(R3 ) and remain at radii comparable to R. The same estimate permits replacing comparable

asymptotic coordinates. This argument uses the original invariant ADM flux, not the mass of a
deformation.                                                                                         □

and QB r−2 f ∂r . Thus neither charge sign has been changed by the identification.
Proposition 11.5 (Global realization of the original data). The original stationary section z = 0
gives a smooth orientation-preserving spacelike embedding of all of Ω into one Reissner–Nordström
exterior together with its future horizon or bifurcation sphere. It induces the original (g, K, E, B),
including their values on S, and its end approaches the corresponding spatial infinity.
Proof. The global product in Equation (11.2) and Proposition 11.4 identify the right wedge with
the static Reissner–Nordström exterior, with the form in Equation (11.10). The original section is a
global graph there by Lemma 11.1. All original tensors are already induced by G, F on that section;
in particular its second fundamental form is the original K, not the zero second fundamental form
of Σ′ . Its unit normal need not be the static normal. The contraction identities defining the original
fields consequently recover both full fields even when they are nonradial or nonparallel on that
section.
   It remains to verify smoothness and injectivity at the original boundary. On the open original
orbit base write
                 G = −W (dz − As )2 + hs ,            As = W −1 Xg♭ ,      dT = dz − As .       (11.11)
The last equality is global: T is the Killing-flow coordinate based on the orthogonal global section
Σ′ , so T − z is a single-valued function of the orbit label. On the original section it says dT = −As .
Let r∗ be the Reissner–Nordström tortoise coordinate, dr∗ /dr = f −1 . Since f ′ (rh ) = 2κ and
W = f (r),
                                       r∗ = (2κ)−1 log W + a(W )                                 (11.12)
86                                             OPENAI


for a smooth function a near W = 0. To verify this expansion, use the smooth inverse r = r(W ) of
f near rh and subtract (2κW )−1 from dr∗ /dW ; the difference is smooth. The normalization of κ is
the one from Proposition 9.10, equivalently the surface gravity of the now normalized static Killing
field.                                                                         √
   If u|S = 0, Proposition 10.4 gives smooth As and a smooth defining function W in the original
collar. Hence T |z=0 extends smoothly there. The orbit metric is a smooth nondegenerate metric
in that collar, and the base isometry extends smoothly by its normal geodesic collars; its angular
boundary label is a diffeomorphism. The exterior Kruskal coordinates
                                 U = −eκ(r∗ −T ) ,       V = eκ(r∗ +T )
                                                     √
are, by Equation (11.12), smooth multiples of W on this section. Both vanish simply in its
inward normal parameter, while the angular variables parametrize S. Their derivative vector is a
nonzero spacelike direction in the normal two-plane, since the two nonzero factors have opposite
signs. This gives a smooth immersion through the bifurcation sphere.
   The exterior in these Kruskal coordinates has U < 0 < V; the attachment coordinates of
Section 10 have U, V > 0.
   If u|S > 0, the other part of Proposition 10.4 gives W as a smooth original boundary defining
function and
                       Xg♭ = (2κ)−1 dW + W β,       As = (2κ)−1 d log W + β,
where β is smooth in (W, y). Equations (11.11) and (11.12) show that the advanced time
                                             v = T + r∗
has smooth differential dv = −β + a′ (W )dW on the original section, and hence has a finite smooth
extension to S. In these coordinates the spacetime metric is
                                      −f dv 2 + 2 dv dr + r2 σ2 .
The sign of the logarithmic cancellation therefore selects the future horizon, where r = rh and v is
finite.
   Here the angular map also
                          √ is smooth √   in the original defining function W , a fact that requires
more than smoothness in W . Put s = W . The formula for Xg♭ shows that
                                                                     2
                                 hs = g + W −1 (2κ)−1 dW + W β
extends as a smooth nondegenerate tensor in (s, y) invariant under s 7→ −s. At s = 0 its normal
coefficient is κ−2 and its tangential metric is g|T S . Reflection is consequently a local isometry
fixing the boundary. In normal geodesic collars the angular labels are constant along the normal
geodesics and invariant under this reflection. The angular components of the base isometry are
thus smooth even functions of s. Taylor’s formula with parameters makes every such function
smooth in s2 = W on the one-sided collar. The radial coordinate r = r(W ) is smooth as well. We
have obtained a smooth map in the regular coordinates (v, r, angles), and dr/dW = (2κ)−1 at S is
nonzero. Together with the boundary angular diffeomorphism this proves immersion at the future
horizon and shows that S maps onto a full horizon cross-section.
   In either case the limiting pullback of the ambient metric is the original smooth positive definite
g, so the immersion is spacelike up to S. The induced future normal is a smooth algebraic
function of its first derivatives and the ambient metric; it therefore extends smoothly as well. The
second fundamental form and the two field contractions agree in the open exterior and extend by
smoothness, proving their equality with the original tensors on S.
   The open graph is injective, its boundary angular map is injective, and the boundary image
is disjoint from the open exterior. Finally its end escapes properly: the base radius tends to
infinity and the rest-chart graph has the original strictly spacelike asymptotic plane slope, up to
sublinear errors. Its image approaches spatial infinity in that end. Compactness of the remaining
                              CHARGED SPACETIME PENROSE INEQUALITY                                      87


core and the boundary collar then make the injective immersion proper, hence an embedding.
The spatial orientation was fixed in Proposition 11.4; flowing the future normal preserves the
corresponding spacetime orientation. This proves the stated orientation and all the required
original-data matching.                                                                     □
Proof of Theorem 2.5. The equality variation and stationary construction in Propositions 9.8, 9.10,
and 10.9, together with Lemma 9.9 apply to the original equality data. Proposition 11.2 proves
static electrovacuum in their entire right wedge. Lemma 11.3 preserves the original invariant mass,
and Proposition 11.4 identifies its orbit metric, lapse, and rotated electric field. The inverse duality
formula (11.10) recovers the prescribed two charge signs. Proposition 11.5 then realizes the entire
original data, with smooth future normal and fields at its horizon boundary. The vanishing of
original non-electromagnetic matter was proved in Proposition 11.2.                                   □

pointwise on each sphere. Taking C ′ |v| < 1/2 proves the claim.
   To deduce the required outermostness, suppose a compact smooth enclosing surface entirely
in the open graph had θ+ ≤ 0. At a point of largest r it is tangent to the coordinate sphere,
with the same outward normal: the radially outward side is in the component containing infinity.
The inner surface’s local graph lies on the inner side of the coordinate sphere. In coordinates on
their common tangent plane, the second derivative test and the mean-curvature principal part
−∆ give Hsurface ≥ Hsphere at contact. Their tangential K traces are equal there because their
tangent planes coincide. Hence its expansion is at least the strictly positive sphere expansion, a
contradiction. The argument applies to a largest-radius point among all components, so it also
excludes disconnected enclosing weakly outer-trapped surfaces.

timelike graph normal gives both fields O1 (R−2 ). Their stress, and hence µ and |J|, are O(R−4 )
and integrable. The compact core causes no integrability issue. Together with Equation (11.15), the
cut calibration, and the expansion barrier, these checks establish every initial-data and connected
rigidity-subclass hypothesis for all three families. The converse just proved supplies equality. □
